We will release our datasets, code, and SAF models.

new TAP: Tapping the Discriminative Potential of Frozen Representations for Class-Incremental Learning

Authors: Zihao Wang (Shandong University), Yu-Wei Zhan (Zhejiang University of Technology), Xiao-Qian Liu (Shandong University), Zhen-Duo Chen (Shandong University), Weitong Liu (Shandong University), Xin Luo (Shandong University), Xin-Shun Xu (Shandong University)

Abstract: Pre-trained models provide transferable representations for class-incremental learning (CIL). However, sequentially adapting these representations introduces optimization costs and may disrupt old-class feature structure. This motivates us to explore whether the discriminative potential of these representations can be further exploited by continually accumulating class information while keeping the encoders frozen. To this end, we propose TAP, a new and novel framework for tapping the discriminative potential of frozen representations in class-incremental learning. It accumulates visual and semantic class information in the frozen CLIP embedding space with only lightweight optimization. TAP comprises three modules: class-conditional visual distribution modeling, class-adaptive semantic calibration, and visual-semantic posterior fusion. The first module retains class means and within-class scatters, combining class-specific and shared covariances to stabilize distribution estimation. The second module learns class-specific weights over text templates from current-task images, adapting text representations to downstream classes. The third module combines the visual and semantic posteriors through a weighted product of experts for prediction over all seen classes without retaining images from previous tasks. Across nine datasets under two incremental protocols, TAP achieves state-of-the-art performance, attaining the highest average incremental and final-stage accuracies among the compared methods. The code is provided as an ancillary file.

new PRIO: Pre-trained Representation for Interactive Objects in 4D Worlds

Authors: Shixun Huang, Lifu Wei, Yue Chang, Chen Geng, Heng Zhang, Jinhyung Ahn, Caoliwen Wang, Siyuan Chen, Donglai Xiang, Jiajun Wu, Peter Yichen Chen

Abstract: 3D worlds can now be captured, reconstructed, and generated at scale, yet the objects in them stay still: a pressed pillow does not compress, and a pulled door does not swing. Bringing these objects into interactive 4D world models requires a representation of how they can move, deform, and respond physically to user actions in real time. We present PRIO, a pre-trained representation for interactive objects. Given static geometry, PRIO predicts an object-specific configuration space in a single forward pass. This space provides generalized coordinates for Lagrangian mechanics, allowing physical laws and user actions to govern motion. Configurations are linear in these coordinates, allowing the quantities needed by the dynamics to be precomputed. Dynamics then run at interactive rates without further network inference. Pre-trained on diverse 4D trajectories, PRIO learns priors over object configurations that generalize to unseen shapes. It supports meshes, point clouds, and 3D Gaussians, bringing objects in reconstructed and generated scenes into interaction. We demonstrate its use in real-to-sim workflows and robot manipulation. In addition, compared with existing 4D generation methods, PRIO generates dynamics faster and follows user actions more faithfully.

new Carry the Fine Print: Budget-Aware Visual Token Compression for Document OCR

Authors: Yixuan Huang, ShengFan Zhang, Jiawei Chen

Abstract: Visual token compression often treats salience or similarity as a proxy for transcription importance. Complete document OCR breaks this assumption: small symbols and repeated entries can be indispensable to the transcript despite being visually inconspicuous. We present Select and Compensate, a two-stage framework built on a simple idea: choosing where to retain a token need not determine all the evidence it carries. Selection combines adaptive encoding resolution with global importance and local contrast to choose which tokens to retain as anchors. Compensation enriches these anchors with nearby discarded content and its relative position, without adding tokens. The OCR backbone remains frozen, and native coordinates and structural tokens are preserved. We evaluate complete page transcription across five backbones on OmniDocBench and document reconstruction on MPDocBench. With half the original visual tokens, our input-adapted configuration reaches 95.42 OmniDocBench Overall on Ovis versus 96.09 for the uncompressed model, and paired evaluations show gains over simply reducing the input resolution on both benchmarks. With the original input grid, our method stays within 0.17 points of the uncompressed model at 75% retention and exceeds a learned global selector by 4.70 points at 25%. On a fixed 200-page Ovis workload at the same 50% visual-token budget, only 3 of its outputs hit the length limit, versus 30-34 for the evaluated RTPrune, DivPrune, and HiPrune adaptations, and it finishes the batch 35-40% sooner. Code will be made publicly available.

new Persona Lenses: What Makes a Vision-Language Model See Through Its Persona's Eyes?

Authors: Yoonho Shin

Abstract: Vision-language models have been used to simulate where a user looks on a web page. However, a persona in the prompt reaches the simulated gaze only partly. We measure this reach as the difference in one model's gaze between two MatrAIx personas that differ in a single attribute. We introduce persona lenses, which place the persona between the model and the page: a text lens (a two-step prompt in which the model first writes what the persona would look at), a pixel lens (marks on the region matching the persona's interests) and a weight lens (a dwell reward gated to keep the scanpath natural). Using Qwen3-VL (4B and 32B) and Qwen3.5-9B on generated and live pages, we compare the lenses with one another and with scale, the 32B model with no lens. It has been confirmed that the text lens separates the personas strongly but is not robust to profile changes that should not move gaze. The pixel lens separates the personas less but is unaffected by attributes unrelated to gaze. At 4B and 9B, the weight lens separates the personas on interests held out from training, with no extra step on a new page. The 32B model separates the personas with the base prompt alone. Therefore, the choice of a lens depends on cost and model size.

new Mesh-Free Keypoint Learning for Category-Level 6D Object Pose Estimation

Authors: Junghoon Seo, Hakjin Lee, Jaehoon Sim

Abstract: Category-level 6D pose estimation relates partial RGB-D observations of unseen instances to a canonical frame shared across a category. Object-aware reconstruction helps adaptive keypoints retain the geometry of the visible surface. Common reconstruction targets select observed points by their distance to a registered instance surface. We present MORSEPose, which derives this support directly from standard pose and metric-size annotations. We map observed 3D points to normalized object coordinates and retain those inside a tolerancedilated pose-and-size envelope. This construction is consistent across camera frames and metric scales. When the annotated extent bounds the object surface, the envelope conservatively contains the support of a conventional surface-distance selector. We integrate this target with multi-level RGB-D features, adaptive keypoints, and learnable category context. Experiments on REAL275 and HouseCat6D show that the envelope closely matches surface-based supervision at both point and task levels, remains stable under boundary perturbations and viewpoint changes, and supports competitive category-level pose accuracy across benchmarks.

new Multi-View Relighting with Mergeable Radiance Fields

Authors: Yinan Wang, Rohan Kadkol, Hyunjin Yoo, Jean-Fran\c{c}ois Lalonde, James J. Clark

Abstract: We propose MergeableRF, a method for radiance field relighting through model parameter merging. Given multi-view images of a scene under known basis lights, our goal is to relight the scene by directly combining the parameters of radiance fields trained under these lights. This is motivated by the linearity of light transport: the image under a mixture of lights is the same weighted combination of the images under each light. However, combining the parameters of independently trained radiance fields does not produce the corresponding combination of images. To address this, we represent a scene with shared geometry and a separate appearance network for each basis light. We start these networks from the same initialization and introduce merge-aware training, which teaches their parameter combinations to reproduce the corresponding combinations of images. Given a target HDR environment map, we compute its basis-light coefficients and use them to merge the appearance networks into a single relit model. Our method supports both Neural Radiance Fields (NeRFs) and 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS), without material estimation, additional relighting networks, or target-specific optimization. Unlike image-space blending, it requires only one rendering pass per viewpoint. Experiments on synthetic Neural Gaffer objects and real OLATverse captures demonstrate relighting quality competitive with methods trained on the same multi-light observations.

new Contrast Where It Matters: Visual Evidence-Guided Decoding for Mitigating Hallucinations in LVLMs

Authors: Yiheng Li, Wenhao Wang, Xingyi Li, Mingda Jia, Zichang Tan, Yinhang Tang, Zhen Lei, Yang Yang

Abstract: Despite strong multimodal capabilities, large vision-language models (LVLMs) often hallucinate when linguistic priors outweigh visual evidence or when predictions are grounded in irrelevant or misleading image regions. Training-free contrastive decoding mitigates hallucinations by contrasting the original prediction with an auxiliary one that exposes linguistic priors. Yet existing methods focus on altering the auxiliary input or computation and do not ensure that either branch uses query-relevant visual evidence during decoding. We propose Visual Evidence-Guided Decoding (VEGD), a training-free framework that identifies query-relevant visual evidence and guides its use throughout contrastive decoding. VEGD locates this evidence by aggregating localization heads with strong visual responses and coherent spatial structure, then selectively weakens it in the auxiliary image. During generation, VEGD directs visual-carrier heads in both branches toward the same evidence location, strengthening access to intact evidence in the main branch while anchoring the auxiliary branch to its weakened counterpart. An adaptive distribution fusion strategy then combines or contrasts their predictions according to distribution divergence. Experiments across diverse LVLMs and benchmarks demonstrate that VEGD consistently reduces hallucinations.

new Support-Guided Triadic Decoding for Hallucination Mitigation in Multimodal Dialogue with LVLMs

Authors: Yiheng Li, Wenhao Wang, Xingyi Li, Zhilin Tang, Zichang Tan, Zhen Lei, Yang Yang

Abstract: In multimodal multi-turn dialogue, dialogue history plays a dual role: reliable responses provide useful context for subsequent turns, whereas hallucinated responses can propagate errors across turns. Existing training-free methods globally adjust the effect of dialogue history on generation, making it difficult to suppress misleading information without weakening useful context. To address this trade-off, we introduce Support-Guided Triadic Decoding (SGTD), a training-free framework that selectively controls the influence of unreliable history and fuses complementary predictions during decoding. Specifically, Historical Answer Support Estimation measures the visual support of historical responses against type-matched alternatives and identifies low-support content that may mislead later generation. Support-Targeted Attention Intervention then weakens the influence of this content while preserving the surrounding dialogue, producing a support-refined prediction. Triadic Decoding combines this prediction with the full-history prediction and answer-free visual evidence, while the Support-Adaptive Plausibility Constraint filters implausible tokens using visual agreement and historical support. Experiments on MMHalSnowball and VisDiaHalBench across representative LVLMs show that SGTD consistently achieves the highest accuracy under hallucinated histories while preserving the benefits of factual context.

new Query-Aware vs. Query-Agnostic Training-Free Video Token Pruning in Multimodal LLMs

Authors: Divya Kothandaraman, Timo Kumkel, Jaclyn Pytlarz

Abstract: Video Large Language Models process large numbers of visual tokens, making token pruning important for efficient inference. Training-free pruning methods differ in whether and how they use the user query, and the value of query conditioning remains unclear. We present a controlled study of training-free, pre-LLM video token selection. Using a shared Qwen-3-VL backbone, token budgets, and decoding settings, we compare 21 pruning principles spanning query-agnostic, shallow query-conditioned, structured query-conditioned, and holistic strategies. To instantiate the last two categories, we introduce Structured Query Pruning, which decomposes the question into semantic anchors, and a Pareto variant, which combines anchor relevance with query-agnostic visual diversity. Across Video-MME, MVBench, and LongVideoBench, relevance computed from the raw question embedding is unreliable (8--16 points below the best methods), whereas anchors derived from query decomposition avoid this failure. However, query access is not inherently advantageous: strong query-agnostic coverage methods remain competitive or superior in the full-field comparison. Relative to a redundancy-only baseline, structured selection helps most on evidence-specific and long-context tasks at tight budgets; on broad tasks, the holistic selector improves over either relevance or redundancy alone. Further, pruning reduces cost and memory. These results support a practical design rule: preserve visual coverage by default, and add structured relevance when the task and token budget demand selective evidence retrieval.

new Anchored Inertial Proposals with Objective Verification for Diffusion-Prior Inverse Problems

Authors: Minwoo Kim, Seunghyeok Shin, Dabin Kim, Hongki Lim

Abstract: Training-free diffusion priors are powerful for inverse problems, but measurement guidance during reverse sampling is local: aggressive updates can improve immediate data fit while disrupting later denoising. We introduce an anchored inertial correction rule that separates proposal generation from acceptance. At each corrected step, the denoiser prediction anchors a frozen clean-space objective combining measurement fit with a noise-level-dependent proximal term. Heavy-ball dynamics with step size and momentum computed from the measured curvature generate a clean-state candidate; a separate verifier evaluates it with the same objective, with the denoiser prediction retained as fallback; both stages are derived from one objective for the whole trajectory, considered one step at a time. The accepted clean state is re-noised to continue sampling. The rule applies to pixel and latent diffusion with differentiable measurement operators, requires no retraining, and adds no denoiser calls inside the correction loop. We analyze how objective verification yields local nonincrease and displacement control. Across natural-image benchmarks, the method achieves competitive reconstruction quality. Code is available at https://github.com/Seunghyeok0715/PIVOT2026.

URLs: https://github.com/Seunghyeok0715/PIVOT2026.

new TwoPass: Draft-Guided Few-Step Video Prediction for Robotic Manipulation

Authors: Yijun Liu, Shijia Ge, Tongtong Feng, Xin Wang, Yuan Meng, Yuwei Liu, Zhi Wang, Wenwu Zhu

Abstract: Action-conditioned video prediction lets robots anticipate the visual consequences of their actions for planning and policy evaluation. Existing predictors denoise each future from noise without using their own estimate of it, which limits the quality of the predicted future, in particular of the manipulated objects, and requires many network calls. We present TwoPass, a self-conditioned training-and-inference framework that keeps the standard flow-matching objective. Self-Generated Draft Training supplies a detached clean-video estimate from the shared network as conditioning at the same noisy state. A Refinement-Only Late Adapter applies a dedicated residual transformation when a draft is present. At inference, Draft-Reuse Iterative Refinement obtains both a solver update and the next draft from each velocity prediction, requiring as few as 4 function evaluations (NFE) per video chunk. Training samples discrete solver-query noise levels and updates the pretrained backbone. Extensive experiments on Bridge and Language Table show that TwoPass achieves state-of-the-art results with 4 NFE, far fewer than the 50-59 calls of existing predictors. Code: https://github.com/PlantPotatoOnMoon/TwoPass.

URLs: https://github.com/PlantPotatoOnMoon/TwoPass.

new UniReFP: Robust Unified Fingerprinting for Vision Models against Cross-Task Repurposing Attacks

Authors: Ziye Geng, Guang Yang, Yihang Chen, Lingfeng Yao, Xing Shi, Miao Pan, Xin Fu, Changqing Luo

Abstract: We identify, for the first time, a new model modification attack--the cross-task model repurposing attack--that can render fingerprints generated by existing model fingerprinting approaches inapplicable. To address this challenge, we propose UniReFP--a novel unified model fingerprinting framework applicable to diverse vision models across different tasks--that utilizes ownership evidence in a shared class-level semantic space rather than original task-specific output space, enabling effective ownership verification when a protected model is repurposed across tasks with heterogeneous output formats. Specifically, UniReFP constructs a surrogate classifier by repurposing the protected model, enabling fingerprints to be generated independently of the original task-dependent output space of the protected model. Moreover, it optimizes grouped fingerprints to encode ownership evidence: fingerprints within the same group are encouraged to induce consistent semantic responses and intermediate representations on the surrogate classifier, while producing dispersed responses on independently trained reference classifiers. During verification, UniReFP uses a task-aware semantic projection mechanism to map heterogeneous suspect-model outputs into unified semantic-presence vectors, and determines ownership by measuring inner-group consistency. Extensive experimental results validate the effectiveness of UniReFP, consistently showing superior performance under cross-task repurposing attacks, commonly studied model modification attacks, and even their combinations.

new Online Mistake Detection in Instruction-Conditioned Procedural Video

Authors: Amit Feldman, Ilan Shimshoni, Rotem Dror

Abstract: An assistant monitoring a person performing a procedure is most useful when it can detect mistakes while there is still time to recover. This requires judging actions against task instructions as the video unfolds, identifying the attempted step, locating it in time, and assessing its execution. Existing online detectors do not use the instruction text, while instruction-conditioned methods operate offline. In this paper, we study online, instruction-conditioned mistake detection in egocentric videos. Using only observed video, task instructions, and prior predictions, our model identifies and localizes completed action attempts and classifies them as correct, modified, slip, corrections, or additions; omitted steps are determined after the video ends. We compare a modular pipeline, with separate step identification, temporal localization, and execution verification, to a joint model that predicts all outputs in a single step. To support supervised learning from mistakes, we repartition EgoPER to include erroneous videos in all splits. The approaches achieve similar step-segmentation F1@0.5 (93.6% and 92.1%), while the joint model achieves higher binary mistake-detection F1@0.5 (84.1% vs. 80.7%), with its largest gain on slips (56.4% vs. 37.5%). Component-level analysis suggests that error propagation between stages contributes to the modular approach's lower detection score. On test videos containing mistakes, both designs outperform released AEM and ESTANet checkpoints under our evaluation protocol.

new VidToC: Rethinking Video Token Compression for Multimodal Large Language Models

Authors: Zihan Xiao (AIM3 Lab, Renmin University of China), Boshen Xu (AIM3 Lab, Renmin University of China), Pei Fu (MiLM Plus, Xiaomi Inc), Jianzhong Ju (MiLM Plus, Xiaomi Inc), Zhenbo Luo (MiLM Plus, Xiaomi Inc), Jian Luan (MiLM Plus, Xiaomi Inc), Qin Jin (AIM3 Lab, Renmin University of China)

Abstract: Extending multimodal large language models (MLLMs) from images to videos faces substantial memory and latency challenges due to the massive number of visual tokens. These challenges have driven the development of numerous visual token compression methods and more efficient LLM architectures. However, existing compression methods are often evaluated with different training setups, tasks, and models, making fair comparison difficult. Meanwhile, the growing use of hybrid Mamba-Transformer architectures raises questions about whether compression strategies transfer across backbones. We present VidToC, a controlled evaluation framework for representative compression algorithms, LLM backbones, and time encoding strategies, covering training-free and fine-tuned settings on three complementary video understanding tasks. Our results reveal task-dependent compression behavior and characterize the effects of compression placement, time encoding, and backbone choice. These findings provide empirical guidance for designing and selecting video token compression strategies across tasks and training settings.

new QFT-Based Hybrid PointNet-Quantum Neural Networks for LiDAR Point-Cloud Classification

Authors: Anurag Kulkarni, Nouhaila Innan, Muhammad Shafique

Abstract: Hybrid quantum-classical learning architectures provide a promising framework for integrating quantum feature transformations into complex data-processing tasks. Among these transformations, the Quantum Fourier Transform (QFT) offers a structured approach to feature processing, motivating an investigation of how its circuit configuration influences predictive performance, quantum resource requirements, and noise robustness. We present a hybrid PointNet-quantum neural network architecture for LiDAR point-cloud classification, combining classical feature extraction, amplitude encoding, and a variational quantum circuit. Five Fourier-processing configurations are systematically evaluated: No-QFT, Full-QFT-After, QFT-no-swap, IQFT-After, and Trainable-QFT. Experiments on KITTI, nuScenes, and PandaSet across three random seeds assess classification performance, class-level behavior, circuit complexity, and sensitivity to the IBM FakeManilaV2 noise model. Under ideal simulation, IQFT-After achieves the highest weighted F1-score on KITTI (0.857 $\pm$ 0.033), compared with 0.838 $\pm$ 0.023 for No-QFT. Removing the final SWAP gates in the QFT circuit reduces circuit complexity while preserving the Pauli-Z measurement features up to a fixed permutation that can be absorbed by the classification head. Under quantum noise, the circuit ranking changes, with IQFT-After exhibiting greater performance degradation and No-QFT and Trainable-QFT showing stronger robustness. Per-class analysis further identifies dataset-dependent differences in minority-class performance. These results demonstrate that Fourier-processing configuration influences both classification behavior and noise sensitivity, highlighting the role of circuit structure and hardware-aware evaluation in hybrid quantum learning for three-dimensional point-cloud data.

new Characterizing Age-Conditioned Affective Reasoning in Vision-Language Models

Authors: Shahriyar Zaman Ridoy, Azmine Toushik Wasi, Lin Gu, Ruogu Fang

Abstract: An age prompt can change how a vision-language model (VLM) rates the emotion evoked by an image. Does the change predict how people of different ages rate that same image? We evaluate 17 VLMs on 900 OASIS images using young-, middle-, and older-adult prompts and estimate image-specific age differences from ratings by 822 participants. Without an age cue, models closely track average human valence (median Pearson $r=0.891$). Under an older-adult prompt, 16 of 17 models assign lower valence on average than under a young-adult prompt. Yet after adjusting for image pleasantness, correlations between model and human age effects range from $-0.030$ to $0.093$, against an estimated ceiling of $0.595$. The discrepancy is clearest on 54 sensitive images rated as pleasant. Across models, the older prompt reduces valence by $0.845$ points on average, while older participants rate these images $0.443$ points higher than younger participants. Providing human ratings from other images improves agreement on the direction of age differences, although image-specific fidelity remains modest on 600 held-out images ($r_{\mathrm{part}}=0.105$). These results show why evaluations of demographic personalization in foundation models need human comparisons at the level of individual stimuli. Project page available at https://shahriyar-zaman.github.io/age-conditioned-emotion-reasoning/

URLs: https://shahriyar-zaman.github.io/age-conditioned-emotion-reasoning/

new LoopedWAM: Looped World Action Models for Elastic Inference

Authors: Anh Dao, Xinxin Liu, Hang Yu, Jeffrey A Chan Santiago, Yuzhang Shang

Abstract: World action models (WAMs) couple generative video modeling with robotic action prediction but typically require large video and action transformers, limiting deployment efficiency. We present LoopedWAM, a compact WAM that recurrently applies a shared six-block core in each expert, executing 30 transformer layers while storing only 12 blocks per expert. To account for their asymmetric inference costs, the video and action experts can operate at different depths: visual features are computed once per query, whereas action computation repeats at every sampling step. We further propose elastic self-distillation, which trains a single elastic checkpoint, LoopedWAM-E, across action-depth and sampling-step budgets. With only 0.58B policy parameters and no robot pretraining, LoopedWAM achieves 96.1% success on LIBERO, 91.9% on RoboTwin 2.0, and 52.2% on RoboCasaGR1, while using $7.4\times$ less peak GPU memory than Fast-WAM under matched inference measurements. On LIBERO, LoopedWAM-E achieves 96.0% success in 27.6 ms or 95.2% in 24.7 ms on an H100, providing multiple accuracy-latency operating points from a single checkpoint; with two sampling steps and four action loops, it stays within 0.5 points of LoopedWAM on all three benchmarks. Ablations demonstrate that weight-shared depth preserves competitive performance, while aggressive action-depth reduction requires explicit budget-aware training. On a real AgileX PiPER arm, LoopedWAM succeeds in 71.3% of 80 trials across four tasks.

new CaT-Movie: Balancing Global Planning and Local Rendering for Long Multi-Shot Video Generation

Authors: Jinpeng Lu, Haoyuan Shi, Yuanshen Guan, Jiyuan Xia, Bin Wu, Yinda Chen, Zhiwei Xiong

Abstract: Current multi-shot video generators render only limited durations per pass, requiring long movies to be stitched from local segments across which narrative progression and visual identity drift. To synthesize long-horizon movies with Coherence across Time, we present CaT-Movie, a multi-shot generation system consisting of a global story planner and a local window renderer. The planner expands user requests via a large language model to preserve, infer, and complete story details, while deterministic code maintains the resulting storyboard across shots to compile self-contained shot prompts and index reference frames from generated history. To execute this guidance, the renderer applies reference-routed sparse self-attention and shot-wise cross-attention to route identities from the indexed frames while isolating per-shot textual conditions. Given the scarcity of high-quality open-source multi-shot data, we construct a dataset of over 350K film shots annotated with semantic state transitions, entity grounding, and multi-granularity requests. We further build CaT-MBench, a benchmark on held-out films with the same annotations. It gives every baseline its native format and counts cross-shot consistency only when a cut opens a new camera setup, preventing repeated framing from passing as consistency. On CaT-MBench, CaT-Movie attains the highest average score and the most consistent characters among all methods. The project page is available at https://jinplu.github.io/CaT-Movie/.

URLs: https://jinplu.github.io/CaT-Movie/.

new DAWN: Robust Neural Blind Deconvolution via Alternating Wiener Guidance

Authors: Yida Pan, Yixuan Jia, Chaoyan Huang, Qing Qu, Ismail Alkhouri

Abstract: Blind image deconvolution (BID) is an ill-posed inverse problem that aims to jointly recover a sharp image and blur kernel from a single degraded observation. Under nontrivial and high noise, which is common in photography and scientific imaging such as microscopy, BID remains particularly challenging, as existing methods suffer from noise overfitting and inaccurate kernel estimation. Existing training-data-free learning methods for BID, such as those based on the deep image prior (DIP), often utilize external guidance by including model-based estimates as additional loss terms, which underexploits the information these estimates carry especially under nontrivial noise. To address these challenges, we propose DAWN, a training-data-free DIP framework that alternates between injecting model-based estimates directly into the model input and refining this imperfect estimate with the network's own prior. To provide more reliable kernel estimates for these injections, we apply autoencoding regularization to both the image and kernel networks. On natural and scientific images under varying levels of Gaussian and Poisson noise, DAWN outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both image and kernel quality (achieving up to +4.5 dB over the next best baseline on the Levin dataset at $\sigma = 0.1$). Without any training images, DAWN remains highly competitive with a state-of-the-art diffusion model on FFHQ, despite the latter being trained on a massive number of in-distribution images.

new Physical Laws without Physics Loss? Probing Learned Dynamics in Video World Models through Forced Response

Authors: Yutao Li, Chen Wang, Wangmeng Zuo, Hui Li

Abstract: Do video world models learn physical laws without a physics loss, or merely imitate motion? Overall motion and appearance scores cannot settle it. We investigate this through nonlinear forced response: an excitation shakes the support of a suspended object. We introduce dual visual-numeric conditioning and adapt six image-to-video backbones to predict this motion using only the flow-matching loss. Wan predicts motion accurately and generalizes to unseen excitations, objects, poses, and scenes. Counterfactual tests suggest capabilities beyond replaying training videos. But what laws have they learned? We design three tiers of tests using Taylor expansions of the dynamics and geometry. All six reproduce the linear response, including resonance; five capture higher-order dynamics, including nonlinear softening. Remarkably, softening is barely present in training: only 0.74% of the swing's squared displacement. Extensions to a double pendulum and a rocking block show sustained dynamics are learned more faithfully than brief impacts. Yet learned dynamics may fail to appear when state estimation or propagation goes wrong. We turn these tests and findings into strategies to improve video training and generation.

new RelGeo: Pair-Conditioned Relational Calibration for Frozen Diffusion Correspondence

Authors: Zixuan Dai, Zhiliang Zhang, Daosong Hu, Tang Zheng, Xinkai Liu, Tianyu Lei, Wei Zhan

Abstract: Frozen diffusion descriptors retrieve strong semantic correspondence candidates, yet cosine nearest-neighbor matching can select incorrect locations despite valid high-ranking alternatives. Across DIFT-SD2.1 and DiTF-FLUX on SPair-71k, AP-10K, and SOCO (CC, SOC, Cross-SOC), 41-69% of Top-1 errors retain a PCK@0.10-valid match among the original Top-5 spatial peaks, revealing a persistent candidate-selection bottleneck. We find that descriptor rankings and current-pair relational evidence can favor different candidates: reciprocal diffusion Query/Key associations and pretrained RoMa transport exhibit distinct, partly complementary preferences. To incorporate this evidence into matching decisions, we introduce RelGeo, a Pair-Conditioned Relational Calibration framework expressing heterogeneous relations as shared source-target coordinates. Spectral Relational Coordinates (SRC) encode reciprocal diffusion affinities, while Warp Identity Coordinates (WIC) encode reliability-weighted bidirectional transport endpoints. Their inner products augment frozen descriptor similarity while preserving full-domain cosine nearest-neighbor matching without additional semantic-correspondence training. RelGeo improves all ten evaluations by an average of 5.51 PCK@0.10 points; fixed-candidate reranking and pair-misalignment controls corroborate improved selection and dependence on current-pair relational alignment.

new What One Sample Cannot See: Why Fewer Denoising Steps Can Score Higher on Physics Benchmarks

Authors: Yutao Li, Chen Wang

Abstract: When a storm can head north or south, a forecast down the middle may score better under squared error than either plausible path. The same paradox appears when video generators are tested as world models: fewer denoising steps can raise physics scores even as videos blur or stop moving early. Why? Physics-IQ compares each generated video with one recording, although a scene can have multiple valid futures. An exact decomposition shows its scores can reward reduced variation among generated futures, even when their distribution becomes less accurate. We introduce Kernel Physics-IQ to remove this incentive in expectation, needing only two independent samples per condition. Two-step sampling lowers Kernel Physics-IQ in all five image-to-video models. For CogVideoX, the official score rises by 0.055 while ours falls by 0.131. Changing the sampling schedule reveals further differences official scores miss. In controlled physical systems with known outcome probabilities, our metric favors the true distribution over collapsed predictions. In short, a world model should be judged not only by how closely one video matches what happened, but by how faithfully its predictions represent what could have happened.

new AesDistill: Distilling Generative Aesthetics for Image Color Enhancement

Authors: Chuanzhi Xu, Chengkun Yue, Huiqi Liang, Haoyang Li, Zihan Deng, Qingwen Zeng, Pengfei Ye, Weidong Cai

Abstract: Image color enhancement aims to improve visual appeal, but previous methods often learn to reproduce ground-truth expert targets. These targets reflect the preferences of a few experts and cover a limited range of aesthetic choices. Matching them does not ensure stronger aesthetic quality across different tastes and scenes. By contrast, large generative models learn rich visual knowledge from large image collections and often produce vivid, appealing image edits. However, direct generation can change the source image's semantic structure along with its colors. In this paper, we introduce AesDistill, a framework for distilling generative aesthetics into image color enhancement. We use 4 leading open-source large generative models to produce 64,644 edited images as teacher targets at a cost of about 3,360 H100 GPU-hours, forming the AesDistill Dataset. AesDistill also includes AesDistill Student, a 1.13M-parameter model that learns teacher color changes through confidence-weighted supervision while preserving the source image's semantic structure. We evaluate AesDistill with 12 aesthetic and 4 image-quality assessment models. Every teacher's targets rank above the FiveK and PPR10K expert target means in the joint assessment. Across both datasets, AesDistill Student outperforms all 20 baseline methods and ranks directly behind the 4 teachers. These results demonstrate that distilling aesthetics from generative models enables a compact student to outperform methods trained on expert targets.

new LG-FiLM: Local-Global Mixture-of-Experts Modulation for RAW-to-RGB Reconstruction

Authors: Fei Li, Xilei Cao, Shichang Zhai, Kun Zhan

Abstract: An image signal processor does more than reconstruct an image. It also has to decide how strongly to denoise, how to balance color, and which tone curve maps the scene onto the display, and these settings depend on the scene itself, the curve that suits a backlit sky is not the one that suits an indoor portrait. Most neural ISPs learn a single fixed transformation and apply it to every scene. We instead make the adaptation an explicit part of the network. Our model, LG FiLM, keeps one shared encoder decoder and places a small mixture of affine experts at its bottleneck; the experts scales, shifts, and mixing weights are predicted from local neighborhood evidence together with a global scene descriptor. Reconstruction stays in a single shared pathway and only the processing mode is switched. We evaluate LG FiLM on three datasets, Fujifilm UltraISP, Zurich RAW to RGB, and a COCO derived benchmark built with an inverse ISP. The model reaches the best reconstruction fidelity of the compared entries on the primary benchmark and transfers to the other two.

new TRACE: Attribute Execution for Sequential Image Editing

Authors: Xiyang Zhang, Yuanhe Tian, Hongzhi Wang, Yan Song

Abstract: Given an image and successive user instructions, sequential image editing should return an updated image that satisfies each new request and preserves earlier requirements that remain active. This lets users develop a result without repeatedly restating or repairing their decisions. The difficulty is that a retrieved image can contain a valid color on an obsolete surface, while the current image may lack the newly requested material. Neither reference selection nor a precise object mask alone specifies how to reconcile attributes occupying the same region. We present TRACE, which separates the continuing attribute from its visual carrier and prioritizes local appearance operations under complete-request checks. Active-goal binding identifies what should persist, carrier selection supplies suitable current or newly generated pixels, and tonal calibration reduces tinting near highlights and shadows. Experiments across frozen editors, independent visual readers, new source images, and complete sequential benchmarks support joint color-material recovery and show how local execution preserves the surrounding scene.

new SEAF: Selective Semantic Enrichment and Visual Evidence Fusion for Multimodal Sarcasm Detection

Authors: Faxian Wan, Xiaocui Yang, Yifan Cao, Shi Feng, Daling Wang, Yifei Zhang

Abstract: Multimodal sarcasm detection requires textual, visual, and contextual evidence, particularly when multiple images provide complementary cues. Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can generate auxiliary information from a post's text and images, but their outputs may mix useful background knowledge with redundant visual descriptions. We propose SEAF, a dual-branch framework for selective semantic enrichment and visual evidence fusion. SEAF assigns generated fields to distinct roles: entity background enriches semantic input, while image descriptions supply alignment statistics. The semantic branch combines the original post text, background from a frozen MLLM, and optical character recognition (OCR) text. The aligned visual branch combines global and local visual features with statistics of cross-modal and inter-image similarity. Text-Anchored Smoothed Aggregation (TASA) weights images by their similarity to the original post text while preserving contributions from images with low literal similarity. Summing the branch classification scores integrates contextual semantics with visual evidence. Experiments on the multi-image MMSD3.0 benchmark show that SEAF outperforms all compared methods.

new Minimal Memory for Correctable Visual Continuation

Authors: Xiyang Zhang, Yuanhe Tian, Hongzhi Wang, Yan Song

Abstract: Given past images and known actions, visual continuation generates future frames from one evolving scene state. Keeping this state revisable matters when an object reappears or a later view changes an earlier interpretation. Once past images cannot be replayed, a summary sufficient for current rendering can lose candidates needed after correction. We establish matching sparse and dense bounds on the bits needed for later correction under stable observations and bounded disagreement. For static streams at the stated smoothness and margin scales, correction incurs at least logarithmic overhead in one dimension and polynomial overhead in higher dimensions. Coherent Archive Memory (CAM) stores geometric summaries with exact updates under clipped expansion and coordinate assignments, then uses historical existence constraints to select one state for all requested frames. Exhaustive photographic tests show strict worst-truth image-risk gains from joint witnesses, while controlled neural continuations recover positions hidden in the current view.

new Beyond Entity Recognition: Vision-Centric Knowledge Visual Question Answering

Authors: Jiatong Ma, Yuchen Liu, Longteng Guo, Yichen Yuan, Yuze Wu, Dongze Hao, Ru Zhen, Yanhao Zhang, Haonan Lu, Jing Liu

Abstract: We propose VC-VQA, a large-scale benchmark for vision-centric knowledge reasoning, aiming to systematically evaluate the capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) in fine-grained visual knowledge acquisition and complex multi-stage reasoning. Unlike existing benchmarks that primarily treat visual information as entity recognition and rely on text-based external knowledge, VC-VQA explicitly models the role of visual information by considering both its source (internal vs. external images) and stage of involvement (pre-retrieval vs. post-retrieval). Specifically, VC-VQA requires models to retrieve external visual evidence, identify visual clues before retrieval, and localize, verify, or match retrieved knowledge against the internal image. We further construct a controlled multimodal knowledge base with traceable textual and visual evidence for each sample. We evaluate 21 representative MLLMs under four settings: no external knowledge, with provided evidence, knowledge base retrieval, and open-web search. Experimental results lead to three key findings: current models still struggle with vision-centric knowledge reasoning; fine-grained visual knowledge acquisition remains the primary bottleneck; and agentic retrieval highlights the value of multi-step planning and cross-modal knowledge integration. Overall, VC-VQA provides a more challenging benchmark for evaluating vision-centric knowledge reasoning in MLLMs. Our code and dataset are available at https://github.com/CASIA-IVA-Lab/VC-VQA.

URLs: https://github.com/CASIA-IVA-Lab/VC-VQA.

new D3-Cache: Accelerating Diffusion Models with Dynamic Mode Decomposition

Authors: Yanbo Li, An Xiang, Xiaoxin Hu, Feng Wang, Cheng-zhong Xu, Kejiang Ye, Xitong Gao

Abstract: Diffusion Transformers generate high-quality images and videos, but every sampling step requires a full network evaluation. Training-free caching reduces this cost by reusing or forecasting network outputs across steps, but common approaches use per-model calibrated skip criteria or maintain per-layer prediction histories. We present D3-Cache, which applies Dynamic Mode Decomposition (DMD) to Diffusion sampling: it fits DMD to a short history of exact velocities and forecasts only the velocity that the scheduler consumes. A horizon-aware gate decides when to skip: whenever the network is evaluated, its output is compared with the forecast for that step, and the larger the error, the sooner the network is evaluated again, up to a cap on consecutive skipped steps. D3-Cache requires no training or per-model offline calibration curve and adds negligible memory. It shares one history length and risk tolerance across the four evaluated models. On four state-of-the-art image and video models, D3-Cache achieves speedups of 2.6-3.8x with high fidelity to the base model output.

new LIFT: Lifting Language-Controlled Image Fusion to Free-Viewpoint 3D Scenes

Authors: Jingning Tian, Hao Zhang, Yumin Huang, Zizhuo Li, Xingyu Jiang, Erdun Zhao, Jiayi Ma

Abstract: Multimodal image fusion traditionally generates a single fused image from paired observations at a specific viewpoint, limiting comprehensive scene perception and adaptability to diverse perceptual objectives. To overcome these limitations, we present LIFT, a novel framework for our proposed language-controlled 3D image fusion (L3D-IF) paradigm that extends multimodal image fusion from 2D images to free-viewpoint 3D scenes, enabling language-guided fusion tailored to diverse perceptual objectives. First, LIFT formulates Semantic Modality Binding (SMB), which interprets language instructions to identify semantic targets and their associated modality preferences and grounds these targets in corresponding regions of a shared Gaussian scene, establishing persistent semantic-to-spatial correspondences for consistent fusion control across viewpoints. Second, Language-Conditioned Gaussian Appearance (LCGA) is developed to achieve scene-wide multimodal appearance modeling by combining adaptive multimodal blending with language- and viewpoint-conditioned appearance refinement, enabling spatially adaptive realization of diverse fusion appearances while preserving source fidelity at pure-modality endpoints. Finally, we construct a Multi-View Language-Supervision (MVLS) dataset that associates language instructions and calibrated viewpoints with modulated fused images, providing effective supervision for learning instruction-dependent fusion from finite multi-view observations. Extensive experiments demonstrate that LIFT achieves coherent multimodal fusion across continuous viewpoints, accurate semantic target localization, and consistent regional fusion control under diverse language instructions.

new Slide2PPT: Native PowerPoint Reconstruction with Localization-Guided Refinement

Authors: Baoliang Chen, Xinlong Bu, Lingyu Zhu, Xiangjie Sui

Abstract: Image-to-slide reconstruction must recover both visual appearance and native object structure for subsequent editing. Yet visually faithful charts and tables may lack editable series and cells, and direct code generation couples content prediction to rendering implementation. We present Slide2PPT, a model-agnostic, training-free framework that equips both open- and closed-source vision-language models with native PowerPoint reconstruction capabilities. Through a unified typed interface, the model predicts object records while a fixed renderer materializes their native properties, enabling integration with different backbones without task-specific training or architectural changes. Localization-guided refinement then connects visual discrepancies to object records for correction. Regional updates preserve unselected records, while whole-slide updates address cross-region relationships. Record refinement and targeted object editing support structural and attribute-level changes, with rendered feedback governing iterative acceptance. To evaluate this framework across diverse reconstruction demands, we introduce Slide2PPT-Bench, covering controlled elements, real slides, and scientific figures. Evaluation separately measures file production, rendered agreement, and native-object availability. Experiments across open- and closed-source backbones demonstrate substantially more reliable PPTX generation than direct code generation, improved layout and text recovery on scientific figures, and native chart and table interfaces beyond geometric primitives.

new Calibrate in Context: Efficient Data-Free Quantization from Small Synthetic Pools

Authors: Haowen Hua, Sen Lin, Yaokun Huang, Zeyi Shao, Hao Zhang, Wei Sui, John See, Zeyd Boukhers, Cong Yang

Abstract: We enable accurate low-bit recovery from small synthetic pools by improving both image synthesis and student preparation. A grid with lower local response error can incur greater error in the student's block context. Functional Grid Calibration (FGC) selects activation scales and integer offsets against full-precision responses constructed from the student's current block input. With weights fixed during calibration, FGC evaluates candidates only at their consuming operators, bringing block context to calibration at nearly local-scoring cost. For DeiT and ViT, progressive inversion updates the same images through increasingly deep teacher prefixes. With 1,000 images, the complete pipeline exceeds MimiQ's reported accuracy in all 18 W4A4/W5A5 settings across nine backbones. With 256 images, DeiT-S and ViT-S reach 68.20% and 66.90% ImageNet top-1 accuracy at W4A4 in 26-27 minutes on one RTX 5090. The pipeline outperforms strengthened 1K-image MimiQ by 13.03 and 24.36 percentage points at lower preparation cost and the same recovery-update count. The same low-budget checkpoints retain their advantage on ImageNetV2.

new SkillLens: Action-Guided Dense Visual Dynamics for Proficiency Estimation

Authors: Deokhyun Ahn, Hyukjin Kim, Jae-Ho Han, Bumsub Ham, Heeseung Choi, Ig-Jae Kim, Haksub Kim

Abstract: Human proficiency estimation is challenging because skill is a high-level and often subjective property that is not captured by action identity alone. Existing approaches leverage multimodal observations such as ego-exo video, gaze, pose, and inertial signals, but the gap between sensor-level evidence and abstract proficiency labels remains difficult to bridge. We present SkillLens, a proficiency estimation framework that uses atomic action descriptions as temporal guidance, rather than direct semantic supervision, to localize proficiency-relevant windows for dense visual-dynamics extraction. Each window is summarized into an action-dynamics feature and scored by a high-low prototype margin against higher- and lower-proficiency executions from the same activity-action category. Fusing these ego/exo PDM cues with a global ego-exo-gaze baseline through CORAL ordinal calibration, our method achieves 0.542 accuracy on Ego-Exo4D. Our project page is available at https://skill-lens.net/

URLs: https://skill-lens.net/

new Interpreting DeepStack-Style Vision-Language Models: Do Input or Deeper-Layer Visual Tokens Drive Answers?

Authors: Yucheng Du, Yi Nian, Ruixue Lian, Yue Zhao, Xiyang Hu

Abstract: DeepStack-style vision-language models feed visual tokens to the language model at its input, and again at deeper layers. We call these the main entry and the later entries. What do the later entries add when the model already has the image? We give the entries versions that differ in one object's color and see which one the answer follows. On photographs, the original DeepStack follows the later entries, while Qwen3-VL-8B/32B follow the main entry. Does weakening the main entry give the later features more influence? On synthetic images, we keep this conflict and reduce color saturation at the main entry only. The unchanged later features gain a stronger effect on the relative scores of the two colors, in all four models, including Granite Vision. This holds even under mild desaturation, when accuracy without the conflict stays at 100%. To test whether later features can also correct errors, we degrade both entries and restore only the later ones. The same blur costs both Qwen models 50 percentage points of accuracy on stripe orientation. The 32B model recovers 48 points and the 8B model only 8. Accuracy with one clean entry predicted which entry each model can lose, and five of six tested predictions held on new GQA attribute questions. With the other entry zeroed, Qwen3-VL-8B loses only 0.3 points on its main entry alone, and DeepStack at most 3.45 points on either entry alone. In split inference on these questions, DeepStack can then send only its main-entry features at higher precision. This takes 49% fewer bytes than all entries at a shared precision, at similar accuracy.

new SteerTrace: Tracing How Target Attacks Steer World Action Models

Authors: Yukun Dai, Tianshi Wang, Fengling Li, Jingjing Li, Xin Li, Lei Zhu

Abstract: World action models (WAMs) predict future videos and actions with one backbone, and their imagined future is often seen as a safety signal. We introduce SteerTrace, a targeted universal attack on WAMs that also traces its effect inside the model. One $\ell_\infty$-bounded (4/255) perturbation per model, shared across frames, tasks, and camera views, pushes the executed action toward an attacker-chosen target. On eight WAMs from four families (FastWAM, Faster-WAM, OpenWAM and LingBot-VA) on LIBERO and RoboTwin, it drives task success close to zero and moves the attacked action toward the target. Same-budget noise and BadWAM produce little action shift in the target direction. We use the signed targeted effect to measure how much of the effect each intervention removes or reproduces. Under the standard attack, restoring future tokens removes 61-94% of the targeted effect in the five models whose action experts attend to them. When the attack is constrained to keep the predicted future close to the clean prediction, the targeted effect can become more dependent on current-frame tokens. In three of these five models, restoring current-frame tokens removes 87-99% of the effect, compared with 9-15% for future tokens. In six models, restoring just 0.36% of the FFN neurons in layers 10-29 of the video expert removes 72-96% of the targeted effect. Residual directions 2-16 hold only 11-18% of the shift energy, yet removing them cancels most of the targeted effect.

new DACA: Dual-Anchor Collaborative Agent for Zero-Shot 3D Visual Grounding

Authors: Jiaming Liu, Linghe Kong, Guixu Zhang, Huaxi Huang

Abstract: 3D visual grounding (3D-VG) is a task that bridges the natural language and 3D space, serving as a foundational capability for embodied intelligence and human-computer interaction. Although recent 3D-VG agents overcome the reliance on pre-processed 3D point clouds, they essentially employ a unidirectional, pipeline-style tool-calling architecture. This architecture separates semantic understanding from geometric reconstruction, leading to error accumulation and an inability to autonomously recover from failures. To address this flaw, this paper proposes the Dual-Anchor Collaborative Agent (DACA), which reinterprets 3D-VG as a semantic-geometric dual-anchor collaborative reasoning paradigm. DACA comprises two complementary specialized tools: the semantic anchor tool holds the visual semantic features of the target and achieves efficient target tracking via optical flow and VLM local verification; the geometric anchor tool holds the 3D spatial geometric features of the target and performs global spatial reasoning via voxelization and multi-keypoint projection. The two components exchange information bidirectionally via a standardized tool call protocol, autonomously deciding on the next perception-action step to form a closed-loop optimization. Experiments on the ScanRefer and Nr3D benchmarks demonstrate that zero-shot DACA achieves an overall Acc@0.25 of 73.6\% on ScanRefer and 70.4\% overall accuracy on Nr3D, far surpassing the latest zero-shot 3D-VG methods. The code and models are available at https://github.com/liujia99/DACA

URLs: https://github.com/liujia99/DACA

new Verifying Before Enhancing: Target Evidence Chain Verification for Infrared Small Target Detection

Authors: Jiayi Zuo, Songwei Pei, Shuhuai Wang, Yifei Wu, Shangguang Wang

Abstract: Infrared small target detection is essential for early warning, surveillance, remote sensing, and space-based target perception. However, infrared small targets are typically tiny, weakly textured, and boundary-blurred, making them difficult to distinguish from complex backgrounds. Moreover, target-like background structures can produce salient responses across multiple scales, making response strength alone insufficient for reliable target discrimination. To address this challenge, we propose VBE-UNet, a Target Evidence Chain Verification Network that assesses candidate reliability beyond response magnitude through complementary evidence cues. Specifically, counterfactual background explanation models local target evidence, while joint evidence verification integrates cross-scale support and perturbation-based contribution verification to guide feature recomposition. The resulting reliability estimates guide selective feature refinement, promoting target-relevant responses while reducing unsupported background activations. Extensive experiments on IRSTD-1K, NUDT-SIRST, and NUAA-SIRST demonstrate that VBE-UNet achieves superior detection accuracy and lower false-alarm rates compared with state-of-the-art methods. Ablation studies and evidence reliability analysis further validate the effectiveness of the proposed verification framework.

new What's in the box? Revealing Object Layouts Inside Opaque Boxes via Contactless Vibration Excitation and Sensing

Authors: Ethan Turok, Matan Kichler, Shai Bagon, Mark Sheinin

Abstract: Can we reveal what is inside an opaque box by observing its surface vibrations? Objects inside a box alter its mechanical response, providing cues about their hidden locations. We introduce a method that uses these cues to predict a box's 2D occupancy layout entirely remotely. A nearby speaker excites vibrations, which we measure on one visible wall using a grid of laser points and a camera measuring each point's speckle-pattern shifts. Our model, WhisperBox, learns to encode these vibrations into a latent representation and decode it into an occupancy map of the box's contents. The method requires no physical contact to excite or sense vibrations. We demonstrate layout recovery across various boxes and single- and multiple-object scenes, with generalization to unseen object instances, positions, orientations, and weights, as well as changes in laser placement and excitation source. Our analysis reveals that informative frequency bands need not coincide with the box's resonances and that the learned latent space captures object placement and supports arithmetic operations. These results demonstrate the potential of remote vibration sensing to infer spatial layouts hidden from view.

new CGM: Chaos-Guided Graph Attention and Expert Routing for Autism Stereotypy Detection

Authors: Hafsa Aslam, Yue Li, Saba Aslam, Abdur Rasool, Qiao Pan, Muhammad Rizwan, Ovidiu Bagdasar

Abstract: Skeleton graph attention for autism video analysis weights joints by content alone, giving an erratic wrist the same standing as a still one. Stereotypy, however, is defined by how unpredictably movement unfolds rather than how much of it occurs: in our cohort, every amplitude-based kinematic descriptor falls in stereotyped clips, while windowed Sample Entropy of angular velocity alone rises (Hedges' g = +1.05). We compute this irregularity as a per-joint, per-frame tensor that structurally drives computation. The proposed chaos-guided graph attention and mixture-of-experts model (CGM) couples two mechanisms. First, a learned pairwise term added to the graph-attention logit concentrates attention on irregular joints and re-ranks the remaining edge weight toward jointly irregular pairs; its unconstrained coefficient neither vanishes nor inverts, and zeroing the tensor realigns attention in 94% of clips. Second, a gate conditioned on the same signal routes each frame to two of four experts, whose weights track frame-level irregularity. Under subject-grouped five-fold cross-validation on 116 clips, CGM reaches 0.930 balanced accuracy, exceeding the graph-attention baseline by +0.187; the component ordering replicates on the public MMASD dataset, and on held-out Panoptic Studio sessions, CGM keeps 0.86 specificity without retraining, against at most 0.35 for chaos-free baselines. In ablation, routing without irregularity yields no reliable gain, whereas chaos modulation alone contributes +0.132. A severity index from the same tensor reaches 0.87 split-half reliability, separates the cohorts with Hedges' g = +1.08, and stays consistent within children across therapy recordings, supporting video-only monitoring of stereotypy during intervention.

new Towards High-Fidelity Learned Video Compression

Authors: Bumyoon Kim, Yongseong Kim, Inhyuk Jeong, Byeungwoo Jeon

Abstract: Professional video workflows operate at qualities that learned video codecs do not reach. We measure how much of a professional quality range a codec covers, from the lowest quality of the standard test conditions, VVC at QP 37, to the highest 4:2:2 grade of the production codec ProRes. To compare inter-frame coding alone, every codec receives original frames as references at the intra positions. On 10-bit YCbCr 4:2:2 test sequences, four recent learned codecs cover 24 to 38% of this range. Their encoders are feed-forward networks with a fixed latent capacity, the number of latent values per sample, of at most 0.25, and removing their quantization raises the quality by at most 1 dB. We propose a learned inter-frame codec whose encoder searches, as the encoders of conventional codecs do. It searches block motion, which a learned network refines, and it searches among learned block transforms whose latent capacity ranges up to 1, selecting the partition, the skip decisions, and the quantized coefficients by a rate-distortion cost. Our codec reaches the upper end of the range on all six sequences. With all residual blocks fixed to 16x16, whose latent capacity is 0.25, the same codec covers 55% of the range, with or without quantization.

new SAM 3 Sees More Than It Detects: Promptable Concept Maps Beyond Categories

Authors: Yu-Huan Wu, Yun Liu, Liangli Zhen

Abstract: Promptable segmenters answer what a thing is. Dense prediction tasks also ask class-agnostic questions, such as what stands out or hides, how light is blocked, transmitted or reflected, and where structure lies. Robots, surveillance systems and image editors build on their answers. We study promptable concept maps (PCM), which extend SAM 3's promptable concept segmentation from noun instances to maps of concepts named by short noun phrases such as `"transparent object", with "none" as an answer. PCM-Bench groups the standard test sets of seven such tasks by the question they ask. On it, we find that SAM 3 sees more than it detects. Its semantic path responds to many of these concepts, yet a low image-level presence score can block every detection of its instance path. We present UNVEIL, which trains a copy of SAM 3's semantic path for these maps, with LoRA on copies of its last blocks. A separate presence decoder learns from verified negatives whether a concept occurs. With one text phrase per query, UNVEIL maps region concepts such as salient and camouflaged objects, shadows, transparent objects and mirrors. The same weights map thin structures such as edges and skeletons as well as object categories. Run next to SAM 3, it leaves SAM 3's own outputs unchanged. On each of the seven tasks, UNVEIL reaches the highest score among the compared promptable models, including SAM 3's official fine-tuning and pipelines built on multimodal LLMs. Code is available at https://github.com/yuhuan-wu/UNVEIL.

URLs: https://github.com/yuhuan-wu/UNVEIL.

new AdaptReg: Skip-level FiLM Adapter for Task-Specific Adaptation of a Frozen Registration Foundation Model

Authors: Shuyue Shi, Rong Yuan, Lin Tian

Abstract: Longitudinal PSMA-PET/CT registration is a key step in assessing treatment response in prostate cancer, and it requires more than generic alignment accuracy: the deformation must preserve lesion volume, so that measured changes in metabolic tumor volume (MTV) and total lesion glycolysis (TLG) reflect treatment response rather than registration artifacts. We propose AdaptReg, which adapts a frozen registration foundation model to such task-specific requirements through a lightweight trainable adapter branch with identity-preserving initialization. Keeping uniGradICON fully frozen, we apply FiLM-style per-voxel affine modulation ($\gamma$, $\beta$) to its decoder skip connections; the modulation is generated from anatomical semantic features of a frozen TotalSegmentator encoder and supervised by a lesion volume-preservation loss. On the official Codabench validation set (20 pairs), AdaptReg achieves DSC 0.7585, HD95 6.33 mm, MTV/TLG errors of 1.68%/2.11%, and 0.0022% non-diffeomorphic voxels, improving on the uniGradICON + instance optimization baseline by $+0.05$ DSC and reducing the MTV/TLG errors by 70.9%/67.7%. new Driver Gaze Target Identification via Gaze Estimation and Structured Driving Scene Understanding

Authors: Xinze Cao, Ke Wang, Deyong Guan, Yuanquan Li, Ke Zhang

Abstract: Identifying a driver's gaze target provides object-level information for visual attention monitoring in intelligent vehicles. Gaze-zone classification and continuous attention localization provide limited information about the identity of the attended scene target. This paper proposes a modular framework that identifies object- or region-level gaze targets from synchronized in-cabin driver images and forward-view road images. The framework comprises four components: MFGaze, which combines a Vision Transformer pretrained with a masked autoencoder (MAE) and head-pose conditioning through feature-wise linear modulation (FiLM); panoptic scene understanding, which constructs a structured candidate set; polynomial ridge regression, which maps gaze features to 2D scene-image coordinates through individualized calibration; and entropy-driven target inference, which adaptively fuses boundary-distance geometry with class-level semantic priors. Experiments evaluate the framework using three public gaze datasets, a laboratory test using Cityscapes road scenes and self-collected gaze data, and the Look Both Ways (LBW) dataset. The complete framework achieves an overall Top-1 gaze target identification accuracy of 96.27% in the laboratory experiment. After sequence-specific calibration, it achieves Top-1 accuracies of 79.92% for task-relevant objects and 85.58% for the road class on LBW. MFGaze achieves the lowest mean angular errors among the compared methods, with 4.06$^\circ$, 4.23$^\circ$, and 10.52$^\circ$ on MPIIFaceGaze, ETH-XGaze, and Gaze360, respectively, and demonstrates strong cross-dataset generalization. Sensitivity analyses show stable performance under hyperparameter perturbations. These results support the framework as a source of object-level gaze information for driver monitoring.

new Suppressing the Shortcut Hidden Behind the Gains of VideoLLM Fine-Tuning

Authors: Youngwoo Shin, Jongsuk Kim, Junmo Kim

Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is the stage in which Video Large Language Models (VideoLLMs) advance their capability to reason over video. However, the VideoLLM also learns to answer from textual regularities of its training data, a shortcut that bypasses the video. The regularities behind the shortcut are hard to define at the data level, and the shortcut forms regardless of training epoch, data size, or backbone. The shortcut is hidden behind a rise in accuracy within the training distribution, yet it is not required for the rise and even costs SFT the gain the video offers under distribution shift. We therefore propose Video-Evidence Training via Orthogonal projection (VETO), which intervenes on the fine-tuning update where the shortcut is written rather than on the data. VETO calibrates the subspace that the text alone occupies in the activations of the base model and projects the update onto its orthogonal complement. The VideoLLM is thus left to ground its answer in the full input, with the shortcut suppressed as it forms and never defined. Across seven VideoLLMs, VETO preserves the gain of SFT on the training distribution and suppresses the shortcut. On six benchmarks under shift it gains more than SFT and answers from the full input rather than the shortcut. Code is available at https://github.com/Youngwoo-git/VETO.

URLs: https://github.com/Youngwoo-git/VETO.

new GeoPillars: A Large-Kernel Pillar Detector for Single-Sweep LiDAR Under Point Sparsity, and a Decomposition of Its Gain

Authors: Chaitanya Dumbre, Shubham Sarkar, Akash Mohapatra, Ghanasham Patil, Sheetal Girase

Abstract: GeoPillars is a pillar-based 3D detector for single-sweep LiDAR under a hard budget of eight points per 0.4 m pillar, a budget that discards 46.6% of in-range points. It adds geometric pillar features, grouped vector attention within each pillar, a learned absolute positional prior on the bird's-eye-view (BEV) grid, a large-kernel backbone, auxiliary surface-normal supervision and a full-resolution detection head. Trained on 123k synthetic CARLA scans and evaluated on cars, it reaches 81.33 BEV AP@0.7, 13.76 points above CenterPoint at the same input budget and 1.91 points above CenterPoint at its native, denser budget. These are in-distribution figures, because the validation split is per-frame rather than scene-disjoint. A rung-by-rung decomposition shows that the new components explain little of the gain. Raising the detection-head resolution alone recovers 10.19 of the 13.76 points, the joint change of encoder, backbone, neck and positional prior adds 3.37, and the normal-supervision branch adds 0.20, which is within run-to-run variation. With heading reversals folded out, orientation error is similar for all seven models, although GeoPillars reverses headings about half as often as a resolution-matched baseline. It is also the slowest of the seven models. Evaluated zero-shot on 6,743 real nuScenes keyframes, no model transfers, and each retains 3.7% to 12.2% of its CARLA accuracy. A collection defect left LiDAR intensity constant, so every model learned from geometry alone. Code and checkpoints are publicly available.

new Fast and Accurate Body Estimation in Pixels, World Space and Scenes

Authors: Jinkun Cao

Abstract: We present FAB (Fast and Accurate Body estimation), a human body estimator that jointly improves inference efficiency and body-recovery fidelity. Rather than predicting only parametric mesh-model parameters, FAB directly predicts human skeleton joints and dense surface points. In images, it predicts dense 2D and 3D body points alongside a parametric Momentum Human Rig (MHR) head, then fits SMPL in one learned pass followed by two Gauss-Newton steps. Its ViT-L student, FAB-L, distilled from a fine-tuned SAM 3D Body, runs in real time for one person on an RTX 4070 Ti SUPER, from detection to a fitted body, at 4.6x the speed of SAM 3D Body's eager-PyTorch body-only path, while improving accuracy on most camera-space benchmarks. The same network predicts heel, toe, hand, and knee contacts, which connect the body to the scene. FAB also estimates world-space motion, fuses static-scene depth around the visible person, and aligns body and scene using predicted contacts. For human-to-humanoid real-to-sim transfer, an in-model G1 head and FAB-Retarget, a motion-level retargeter that reads dense points or the fitted mesh, map motion to a Unitree G1. FAB-Retarget produces references that follow the person more closely than specialized retargeters including SOMA, GMR, UMR, and OmniRetarget, and substantially more closely than Fast SAM 3D Body retargeted with our sequence IK. Project page: https://www.jinkuncao.com/FAB.
