new ScaleWorld: High-Resolution World Generation through Shared Prediction and Refinement

Authors: Yansong Qu, Wesker Zheng, Quanjian Song, Zhiyuan Zhang, Xiao Cao, Shengchuan Zhang, Liujuan Cao

Abstract: High-resolution generation enables video world models to render fine-grained scene structures and textures, but directly generating high-resolution video incurs substantial computational and memory costs. We present ScaleWorld, a causal video model that unifies low-resolution world prediction and high-resolution refinement within a single shared backbone. By refining its own predictions, ScaleWorld produces high-resolution world videos without requiring a separate refinement model. For each video chunk, the shared backbone first completes a low-resolution prediction and then performs one-step high-resolution refinement conditioned on that prediction. To support efficient streaming generation, we maintain separate causal states with a long low-resolution history for prediction and a short high-resolution history for refinement. To address task interference during distillation, we separate specialist optimization from capability consolidation and introduce task-asymmetric on-policy distillation (TA-OPD). This procedure transfers prediction and refinement behaviors from specialized teachers into a shared student through local velocity matching under their respective sampling and history policies. {Extensive experiments demonstrate that ScaleWorld achieves high-quality world generation with improved computational and memory efficiency.

new One Prior, Two Dynamics: Repurposing Video Diffusion Transformers for Self-Supervised Latent Action Learning

Authors: Yuxin Jiang, Wenqiang Sun, Jiaqi Li, Yuchao Gu, Lvmin Zhang, Ivor Tsang, Mike Zheng Shou

Abstract: Videos reveal how the world changes, but rarely the actions that cause those changes. Latent action learning recovers such supervision from visual transitions, yet often learns the underlying dynamics largely from scratch. We ask whether a pretrained video generator can instead provide the prior for both inverse and forward dynamics. We introduce O$^{2}$LaF, built on a simple principle: one prior, two dynamics. We initialize both models from the same pretrained video DiT and adapt them to their distinct roles: a feed-forward transition encoder with learnable action queries for inverse dynamics, and a causal, action-conditioned video generator for forward dynamics. By controlling information access and optimizing both dynamics end-to-end through a compact bottleneck, O$^{2}$LaF learns transferable actions from unlabeled videos. Across navigation and robotic manipulation, O$^{2}$LaF sets a new state of the art in few-shot action probing and enables stronger zero-shot action transfer across visual contexts and embodiments. Beyond generative video priors, we further extend our framework to predictive representation priors such as V-JEPA2, achieving competitive performance. Our findings suggest a broader paradigm for latent-action learning: repurposing pretrained video models to discover transferable actions and predict their consequences.

new RL Post-Training in Diffusion Transformers Is Activation Steering

Authors: Tuna Han Salih Meral, Kaan Oktay, Alec Helbling, Atakan Kara, Duen Horng Chau, Pinar Yanardag

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) post-training substantially improves the visual quality, text rendering, and compositional accuracy of diffusion transformers. Because capabilities such as rendering requested text or composing multiple objects are inherently prompt-dependent, a natural hypothesis is that improving them requires learning complex, input-dependent transformations. To isolate how much of these gains an input-independent shift explains, we probe each RL-tuned model with a deliberately simple parameterization: a fixed set of steering vectors added to the block outputs of the frozen base model. On SD3.5-M, these vectors hold 65x fewer parameters than the RL-tuned LoRA (0.29M against 18.8M). We fit them to match the velocity predictions of public RL-tuned checkpoints, with no reward in the objective. Although the vectors are identical for every prompt, token, and sampling seed, they recover 90.0-96.3% of the reward gains of six RL-tuned image models, and 89.8% and 111.9% on two RL-tuned video models. Initialized at zero and trained with online RL, the vectors alone exceed the reward gains of weight-based RL on text rendering (OCR) and human preference (PickScore) on SD3.5-M. A constant shift thus suffices for most of the gain of policy-gradient RL post-training: without new input-dependent transformations, it steers the pretrained generator toward what it can already compute.

new Posterior Matching Distillation

Authors: Hidir Yesiltepe, Julius Berner, Arash Vahdat, Pinar Yanardag

Abstract: Diffusion distillation accelerates image and video generation by compressing a pretrained teacher's iterative sampling process into a few network evaluations. Distribution-based methods achieve this by matching the student's output distribution to the teacher's. This matching is often driven by reverse-KL objectives, whose mode-seeking bias favors dominant teacher modes at the expense of diversity across samples. We introduce Posterior Matching Distillation (PMD), a new member of the distribution-based distillation family that combines mode-covering posterior sampling with any-step generation. At zero population loss, PMD recovers the teacher's full posterior of clean images at each trained noise level. Its stochastic student supports any-step sampling budgets within the trained timestep grid without further training. To learn these posteriors, PMD matches exponentially reweighted averages of student samples conditioned on a fixed noisy state to corresponding teacher posterior means. Under Gaussian corruption, exponentially tilted posterior mean is a single teacher evaluation at a shifted input. We prove that, matching tilted means over an open neighborhood uniquely identifies the full posterior. PMD thus supervises the entire conditional distribution through a family of mean constraints, without any auxiliary student-score network. Experiments on SDXL and FLUX.2 [klein] 4B demonstrate that PMD achieves the best FID among 4-step methods on both models, while maintaining competitive or leading performance across prompt-alignment and perceptual-quality metrics.

cross TETHER: Training-Free, Elastic, ToC-Anchored Hierarchy Extraction and Recovery in Design-Driven Long Documents

Authors: Yu Zou, Yan Chen, Jialei Zeng, Xiaorui Zhou, Aixi Zhong, Qi Zhou, Haoran You, Ke Jiang, Changyu Hu

Abstract: Converting complex documents into structured knowledge constitutes the critical infrastructure urgently required for deploying Large Language Models (LLMs) in downstream research. However, blind to the Table of Contents (ToC) as a macro-structure, existing bottom-up paradigms rely on localized visual cues, inducing heuristic fragility in high-entropy layouts where visual prominence decouples from logical depth; meanwhile, direct LLM extraction lacks spatial grounding, triggering hallucinations in long contexts. To address this, we introduce TETHER, a training-free framework that elevates the ToC from discarded noise to a vital structural prior, enabling robust adaptation across design-driven long documents. TETHER operates via a two-stage neuro-symbolic mechanism: first, it constructs a document skeleton from the raw ToC using a Large Multimodal Model (LMM) empowered by spatio-semantic constraints; second, it employs an anchor-based alignment strategy to tether content blocks to this skeleton, effectively resolving boundary ambiguities. Evaluated across a multilingual benchmark of ESG reports, product manuals, and annual reports, TETHER delivers highly precise, state-of-the-art (SOTA) zero-shot hierarchy recovery, outperforming deep hierarchical recovery models, dedicated parsers, and general-purpose LMMs. Moreover, stress tests highlight TETHER's resilience to sparse omissions and textual noise, demonstrating that its precise semantic alignment is fundamentally anchored in topological and depth fidelity. Crucially, ablations confirm the framework's architectural irreducibility, while achieving 90% token reduction and drastic speedups over end-to-end baselines, establishing a compelling equilibrium between structural precision and deployment scalability.

cross Density Feedback Control for Transferability of Generative Sparse Adversarial Attacks

Authors: Dongsu Song, Jay Hoon Jung

Abstract: Generator-based sparse adversarial attacks train encoder--decoder networks to produce transferable sparse perturbations for black-box robustness evaluation. Their perturbation density, however, is set only indirectly by a fixed sparsity coefficient whose mapping to density is unknown and shifts during training, so reaching a target density requires re-tuning; this fixed pressure also yields little transferability gain once density reduction begins. We propose Density Feedback Control (DFC), which tightens or relaxes this coefficient at the end of each epoch according to the gap between the observed hard-mask density and the target, without changing the generator architecture or loss. At a 10\% target density, DFC brings the mean density of three base attacks within one percentage point of the target in a single run and improves the transfer attack success rate (ASR) of two of them on CNN and Transformer targets, including under per-image $\ell_0$ budgets, while perturbing fewer pixels. Tracing GPG's training, we observe that as DFC relaxes the coefficient, the transfer ASR recovers with the density and overtakes the fixed-coefficient baseline with fewer pixels. For EGS-TSSA, which restricts perturbations to source-model feature-importance regions, DFC yields no consistent gain; we analyze how this restriction interacts with density feedback.

cross Inhabitant: An Agent Framework for Long-Horizon Human-Scene Interaction with Spatial Control and Quantitative Feedback

Authors: Yao Zhang, Zhuchenyang Liu, Yu Xiao

Abstract: Recent work on human-scene interaction (HSI) generation moves from single interactions to long-horizon interaction, where a language-model agent plans and executes a sequence of interactions in a 3D scene under one instruction. However, existing agents struggle in challenging everyday scenarios (e.g., picking up a toy and throwing it into a basket) because they lack spatial control over the motion and quantitative feedback on its execution, and they often fail to complete the interaction sequence. To address these gaps, we present Inhabitant, a training-free agent framework in which the agent specifies each motion through positions, paths and poses, measures how the executed interaction deviates from its intent, and revises the control specification accordingly. For an extensive evaluation in such scenarios, we build Inhabit, a benchmark of 75 household tasks in 31 furniture layouts of an apartment, in which every task chains one to three goals on movable objects and is carried out by the whole body, excluding fingers. On Inhabit, Inhabitant completes 98.7\% of the tasks with Claude Opus 5.5, and 93.3\% and 82.7\% with Claude Sonnet 5.5 and Gemini 4 Argon, respectively. Ablations confirm that without spatial control the agent fails most tasks, and without quantitative feedback its motion becomes less precise. Code is available on the project page.

cross MAWAM: A Multi-Agent World-Action Model for Coordinated Manipulation

Authors: Xianzhe Fan, Chi-Pin Huang, Fu-En Yang, Jing Wang, Yecheng Wu, Yu-Cheng Chou, Johan Bjorck, Linxi "Jim" Fan, Jan Kautz, Yu-Chiang Frank Wang, Zhiding Yu, Hengshuang Zhao, Zhiqi Li

Abstract: Every move in a robot team changes the world in which its teammates must act. Coordinated manipulation therefore calls for predicting the team's actions together with the physical future they jointly create. We introduce MAWAM, a multi-agent world-action model that jointly generates each robot's future views and executable action chunks in a single sample. Built from a pretrained single-robot world-action backbone, MAWAM jointly denoises all robots' action and future-view tokens through cross-agent attention, while preserving a separate control interface for each robot. MAWAM achieves the highest success on all three real-robot tasks (laundry-bag collection, bath-towel folding, and umbrella handover), with 67.8% mean success, 18.9 percentage points above MA-VLA trained on the same demonstrations and cameras. In simulation, MAWAM reaches 90.3% mean success on eight tasks with teams of up to four arms. Ablations show that cross-agent attention and team-stage future-video supervision both improve coordinated control: without attention, a four-arm relay falls from 95.0% to 39.0%, and without this supervision, Place Food falls from 78.0% to 47.0%.

cross Long-Range Video Presentation Attack Detection: A Three-Class Benchmark Across 1-100 m and Two Sensor Types

Authors: Lambert Igene, Mohammad Zahir Uddin Chowdhury, Afzal Hossain, Sandip Purnapatra, Peter Johnson, Stephanie Schuckers

Abstract: Face presentation attack detection (PAD) systems deployed in border control and airport surveillance are constrained by a critical assumption: subjects are captured at close range, typically under one meter. No existing benchmark systematically addresses multi-distance capture, diverse environmental conditions, and concurrent spoofing and occlusion-based attacks, leaving deployed models underprepared for real-world scenarios. We introduce a video-based PAD dataset of 374,000+ frames from 261 unique identities collected across five standoff distances (1 m-100 m) under indoor and outdoor conditions using a DSLR camera (C) and a mobile sensor (M), with three-class labels: live, spoof, and props (occlusions); models are trained and evaluated on video clips rather than single frames. We fine-tune and benchmark four CNN architectures: MobileNetV3, MobileNet, EfficientNet, and ResNet-50. The higher-resolution DSLR maintains near-perfect spoof classification rate across all distances, while the lower-resolution mobile sensor degrades sharply beyond 50 m, with props classification rate dropping to 55% at 75 m for MobileNetV3. EfficientNet achieves the best overall performance (98.48% accuracy, ACER 2.01%). Generalization tests on unseen attack datasets yield 89-94% accuracy, validating cross-domain robustness. These results establish capture distance and sensor resolution as critical but underexplored axes of PAD difficulty. The dataset will be publicly released under an End-User License Agreement to support reproducible long-range PAD research.

cross GEMS: Generation and Experience Memory for Subgoal-Guided Long-Horizon World Model Planning

Authors: Yangchen Zhao, Ali Payani, Caiwen Ding

Abstract: Visual world models enable effective short-horizon goal-conditioned planning, such as reaching nearby targets and pushing objects over short distances, by predicting candidate action outcomes and comparing their representations with a goal representation. However, extending this approach to long-horizon tasks is challenging: prediction errors accumulate over extended rollouts, while latent-space similarity to distant goals provides limited guidance for ranking candidate outcomes. Existing methods address these limitations by introducing intermediate subgoals, either generated by learned models or selected from experience memory. Generated subgoals can contain hallucinations or be inaccurate in aspects such as object positions and timing. Memory-based approaches are constrained by experience coverage and may lack precise guidance for selecting appropriate intermediate states from stored observations. We introduce GEMS, which generates temporally structured subgoals to guide action search and state selection from Latent Experience Memory (LEM). A distance gate determines whether predicted outcomes are evaluated against the generated subgoal or the selected memory state. Experiments across six tasks and task suites spanning manipulation, navigation, and reaching demonstrate the effectiveness of GEMS for long-horizon planning. GEMS outperforms state-of-the-art world-model planning baselines across different manipulation settings, achieving success rates of 92.8% on Cube, 68.4% on Push-T, and 54.1% on Meta-World. It improves over the strongest evaluated baseline on these tasks by 24.0, 7.3, and 22.2 percentage points, respectively. These results demonstrate that generated subgoals and experience memory can jointly strengthen long-horizon planning with a frozen world model.

cross Bridging vision-language reasoning and learned control for legged navigation in the wild

Authors: Takahiro Miki, Robin Schmid, Manthan Patel, Turcan Tuna, Julia Richter, Julian Nubert, Joonho Lee, Matthias M\"uller, Marco Hutter

Abstract: Autonomous navigation in diverse real-world environments remains one of the greatest challenges in robotics. It demands both robust low-level motion control to handle diverse terrain and high-level reasoning to interpret environmental cues. We present ROAM (Robust Open-world Autonomous Mobility), a system that enables legged robots to perform kilometer-scale navigation in complex alpine and urban settings. ROAM combines the world knowledge of a Vision-Language Model (VLM), pre-trained on internet-scale data, with robust locomotion and local planning policies learned in simulation via reinforcement learning (RL). The VLM provides common-sense reasoning and semantic interpretation of the scene, assessing risks, interpreting signs, and producing semantic labels with associated navigation costs. An open-vocabulary segmentation module converts these labels into a dense, real-time risk map, which conditions two pre-trained RL policies responsible for agile locomotion and local navigation, effectively coupling high-level VLM reasoning with low-level physical control. Real-world experiments showed the system's ability to autonomously navigate a wide range of complex real-world environments. The robot completes kilometer-long hikes with minimal intervention, interprets signs to avoid danger, and evaluates its environment to identify safe areas on which to step. This work represents a step toward the reliable deployment of systems in unstructured environments. However, it also underscores the challenges that remain in VLM reliability and spatial reasoning, indicating that foundational models are a crucial component but not yet a complete solution for navigation in the wild.

cross Reject and Report: Forgery-Evident Undetectable Watermarks for Generative Image Models

Authors: Yihan Wang, Haochen Sun, Xi He, Yaoliang Yu, N. Asokan

Abstract: Provenance schemes such as those based on content watermarks let images from a generative model be traced back to it. A practical watermarking scheme must be accurate at a low false-positive rate (FPR), robust under content-preserving processing, and undetectable. In addition, it must resist forgery; otherwise, even from a single watermarked image and a surrogate model, an adversary can imprint the watermark onto an arbitrary image or regenerate new content that carries it, falsely attributing harmful content to a model or making authentic photographs appear AI-generated. We argue that a watermarking scheme should not only reject forgeries but also report them: a forgery rejected as unwatermarked leaves no trace of the attempt. State-of-the-art forgery-resilient undetectable watermarking schemes, such as SemBind, fall short on both counts. External anomaly detectors that do report forgeries either miss most of them or falsely flag genuine images after common processing. We propose Tripwire-WM, the first forgery-evident framework for undetectable watermarks. It commits the semantics of the generated image to the magnitudes of the initial latent noise, which the sign-based watermarks leave unused, through a distribution-preserving embedding; a forged image carries a valid watermark but with a commitment that does not match its content, so the detector both rejects it and reports it as forged. Built on the pseudorandom code (PRC) watermark and Gaussian Shading++, Tripwire-WM provably preserves their undetectability. On MS-COCO, with the owner's own model as the surrogate, it accepts no imprinting forgery, compared with up to 55.0% for SemBind, and reports all of them as forged; it also accepts at most 1.8% of reprompting forgeries, against up to 46.2% for SemBind. Meanwhile, it accepts at least 99% of genuine watermarked images at 10^{-6} FPR and matches the robustness of the base schemes.

Abstract: We introduce the Multimodal Knowledge Agent (MKA) benchmark, which measures how well autonomous agents perform end-to-end professional knowledge work that depends on visual understanding. cross Breaking Noise Shortcuts in Self-Supervised Learning Via Noise-Aligned View Generation

Authors: Oankar R. Patil, May D. Wang

Abstract: Self-supervised learning (SSL) is increasingly applied to scientific imaging domains, such as microscopy, where labels are scarce but noisy data is abundant. In all settings, SEANA outperforms the denoiser-preprocessed SSL pipelines, demonstrating that learned VST-space noise-aligned resampling yields more robust representations than denoising alone. Abstract: Fine-grained visual understanding requires multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to recognize small visual entities and understand their relationships. cross Stereo Visual Odometry with In-the-Loop Geometric Feed-Forward Models Towards Dense Metric Mapping

Authors: Fahira Afzal Maken, Sagun Man Singh Shrestha, Edward Vanderfeen, Lucas Carvalho de Lima, Rongxin Li, Changming Sun, Milad Ramezani

Abstract: We present a stereo visual odometry (SVO) framework that integrates geometric feed-forward models (GFMs) for dense depth prediction with factor-graph-based visual odometry to achieve accurate localisation and dense metric mapping. Our key idea is to exploit sparse but reliable metric geometry estimated from stereo and temporal multi-view observations to calibrate dense GFM predictions, while retaining the robustness and efficiency of model-based SVO. Rather than directly conditioning the GFM on potentially noisy stereo depth, optimised 3D landmarks are used to jointly estimate a scale-shift transformation over all keyframes within each local submap, yielding metrically consistent dense depth predictions. To balance accuracy and computational efficiency, we introduce a hierarchical factor-graph architecture. At the local level, sliding-window optimisation estimates high-frequency camera poses and multi-view-refined 3D landmarks. Dense predictions are generated only for selected keyframes and registered within each submap using SE(3) Generalised ICP. Consecutive submaps are further aligned in Sim(3) using their shared geometry, allowing residual scale inconsistencies to be corrected. These intra- and inter-submap constraints are incorporated into a global factor graph to produce a consistent trajectory and dense metric reconstruction. Extensive experiments on public and in-house datasets demonstrate that the proposed framework achieves competitive odometry accuracy against geometric and learning-based baselines while providing accurate dense metric reconstruction and improved robustness to failures in learned depth prediction.

cross Life-IQA+: Boosting Blind Image Quality Assessment via GCN-enhanced Layer Interaction and Flexible Hierarchical Expert Specialization

Authors: Long Tang, Guoquan Zheng, Huiyu Duan, Jie Hao, Yucheng Zhu, Liang Yuan, Xiongkuo Min, Guangtao Zhai

Abstract: Blind image quality assessment (BIQA) is essential for evaluating and optimizing visual experience. Existing BIQA methods typically aggregate features from multiple backbone stages without accounting for their unequal contributions to quality prediction. Furthermore, uniformly supervising all experts may constrain their flexibility and limit the diversity of the learned quality representations. To address these issues, we present Life-IQA+, a quality representation decoding framework that combines graph convolutional network (GCN) enhanced layer interaction and flexible hierarchical expert specialization. Specifically, the layer interaction module constructs quality queries from GCN-refined deepest-stage features and uses penultimate-stage features as keys and values, thereby enabling cross-layer representation interaction via the cross-attention mechanism. Building on these representations, we further introduce a flexible hierarchical mixture-of-experts (MoE) architecture comprising a densely activated shared expert layer and a sparsely activated routed expert layer. Experiments on synthetic and authentic distortion benchmarks, together with controlled ablations of expert configurations and supervision strategies, show that Life-IQA+ achieves competitive prediction accuracy with a favorable balance between accuracy and computational cost. The code is available at: https://github.com/TANGLONG2/Life-IQA-PlusPlus.

cross Trajectory-Calibrated Merging of Embodied Policies

Authors: Wenshuo Chen, Honglei Jin, Le Yang, Jianfei Song, Shaofeng Liang, Lei Wang, Junze Zhu, Kailong Wang, Zhengxi Jin, Haozhe Jia, Honglin Chu, Songning Lai, Xiucheng Wang, Yutao Yue

Abstract: Robot foundation models are increasingly adapted into task specialists to expand their embodied capabilities. Yet these capabilities remain fragmented across separate policies, and existing model-merging methods do not reliably preserve them during closed-loop execution: parameter-space alignment provides only an indirect proxy for behavior, while regression may fail to preserve behavior at states not covered by its calibration inputs. We present Trajectory-Calibrated Regression (TCR), an execution-aware, gradient-free framework that consolidates robot specialists into a single policy. Across two complementary timescales, TCR collects calibration inputs from expert rollouts spanning different stages of task execution and from intermediate states within iterative action generation. To account for representation shifts introduced by merging, TCR recomputes each block's input through the already merged blocks. It then uses each expert's computation within the block to form module inputs and fits merged weights to match the experts' linear outputs. A two-pass calibration procedure further refines the merged policy using separate rollout caches while retaining the frozen specialists as teachers. The resulting model preserves the original inference graph and requires neither expert routing nor additional gradient training. On LIBERO, TCR merges four $\pi_{0.5}$ specialists and achieves 77.83% success across 1,200 evaluation episodes, outperforming FeatCal, RegMean, and direct weight averaging by 9.50, 24.17, and 33.00 percentage points, respectively.

cross Bradley-Terry Training Can Distort Outcome Probabilities of Initially Accurate Language World Models

Authors: Xinze Shi, Litian Zhang, Zhenjie Su, Zelong Yu, Yuting Zhang, Qihua Feng, Zifeng Kang, Chaozhuo Li, Zhengxuan Hu

Abstract: Comparative feedback, in which a judge says which of two candidate outcomes is closer to what happened, is a practical way to train language world models that assign probabilities to an action's outcomes. We show that, fitted with a Bradley-Terry objective, it need not teach the true outcome probabilities and can distort accurate ones. On a simulated robot push with enumerated outcome distributions, this objective takes a model that is accurate on unseen layouts (hazard error 2.4 percentage points, pp) and moves 33 to 43 pp of its probability onto outcomes that none of a condition's enumerated branches produces. The cause is the objective, not the comparisons: its optimum, computed before training, predicts where the model ends up, and a likelihood that knows the judge's rule learns from the same comparisons without the shift. The shift is robust: it recurs on unseen conditions, under five candidate laws and a three-fold denser enumeration, and about 29 pp remain without the physically impossible events. Its cost is conditional: with those events it inflates hazard probabilities, and a threshold inherited from the pre-feedback model costs about 3 pp of utility per decision on trained and 1 to 2 pp on unseen conditions, through false rejects; without them the utility loss averages below 1 pp over six seeds, though a small regret in action choice remains. Audits of readout, response and decision trace the effect from probabilities to actions.

cross Erasing the Wrong Thing: Conditional Attribute Leakage and Concept Erasure in Shared Foundation-Model Embeddings

Authors: Haijun He, Fei Li, Donghong Ji, Junjun He

Abstract: Medical-image embeddings are increasingly shared in place of raw images for authorized downstream tasks, yet they may reveal sensitive attributes. Standard concept-erasure methods target population-level guardedness. However, we prove that, when binary task and sensitive labels are associated, global and task-conditional linear guardedness cannot both hold while a nonzero linear task signal is preserved, so global erasure must either eliminate that signal or retain task-conditional linear leakage. We further show how global erasure affects linear readers' discrimination between positive and negative samples with different sensitive attributes, deriving an approximate closed-form prediction of its utility cost from pre-erasure statistics. Motivated by this conflict, we introduce Task-Conditional LEACE (TC-LEACE), a closed-form variant of LEACE that enforces linear guardedness within each task stratum of its fitting data. We evaluate four vision foundation models on 85 CelebA attribute pairs spanning a broad range of associations. After LEACE, conditional leakage persists across all four encoders, the discrepancy between marginal and conditional audits increases with association strength, and predicted utility costs track observed losses. We then examine hospital chest radiographs using three foundation models, including two biomedical models, in which chest-tube status is strongly associated with diagnosis. Although a marginal attack on LEACE-erased embeddings underperforms a diagnosis-only reference, chest-tube status remains detectable within diagnosis strata. TC-LEACE recovers most of LEACE's linear task-utility loss while keeping task-conditional leakage within a prespecified margin of LEACE's.

cross MambaLIFT: Reference-Based Mechanism-Aware Attribution for VMamba

Authors: Jintu Kakati, Niladri B. Puhan

Abstract: Attributing predictions of visual state-space models remains challenging because input-dependent selective dynamics couple feature selection, recurrent state propagation, and shared computational pathways. cross Extending Rademacher Complexity-Based Generalization Theory to Domain Generalization

Authors: Xi Wang, Liang Bai, Xian Yang, Richard Yi Da Xu

Abstract: Domain generalization (DG) aims to train models on multiple source domains with distinct distributions to ensure robust performance on unseen target domains. In doing so, it elevates the concept of generalization from the traditional sample level to the distribution level. Existing theoretical frameworks introduce a domain space endowed with a meta-distribution to extend classical generalization analysis to this new setting. However, these approaches typically treat the domain space as devoid of geometry. In reality, the domain space is an inherently infinite-dimensional, nonlinear abstract space composed of probability distributions, and its complex geometric properties fundamentally determine generalization behavior. Consequently, existing geometry-free theories of domain generalization have a fundamental limitation. They lack a formal characterization of what distinguishes distribution-level generalization from sample-level generalization, leaving the inherent difficulty of generalizing across domains largely unexplained. To bridge this gap, we formalize the domain space as the Wasserstein space over the representation space. Building on the geometric properties of this space (specifically the $\mathrm{CD}(K,\infty)$ curvature theory) we derive a novel, geometry-dependent DG error bound. By introducing an additional curvature-dependent confidence term, this bound formally disentangles the two levels of generalization effects and reveals the essential nature that domain generalization is affected by both the invariance and the curvature of the representation space. Finally, we experimentally validate the effect of curvature on domain generalization.

Abstract: Efficient chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning in vision-language models (VLMs) requires reducing both intermediate reasoning steps and their execution cost. cross Interpretable Prototype-Conditioned Adaptation for Weather Forecasting

Authors: Zhixuan Li, Hao Jiang, Chen Hui, Kai Geng, Weisi Lin

Abstract: Data-driven weather forecasting requires distinguishing local states and their temporal evolution. However, learned patterns remain implicit in neural forecasters, making their influence difficult to interpret. Failing to distinguish local evolution can lead to similar forecasts for locations that develop differently, increasing prediction error. This motivates explicit references for distinguishing and interpreting local patterns. Prototypes provide explicit pattern references, but sample- or task-level conditioning leaves open how to adapt to distinct locations and variable groups. To deal with this problem, we design ProtoCast, which uses group-specific prototype banks to adapt local features while accounting for cross-variable dependencies. Specifically, (1) we combine each group's historical features and temporal change with forecast context from cross-variable attention for evolution-aware prototype matching. (2) Sparse mixtures of prototype-specific low-rank operators tailor corrections to each group and location. A gated attention block then exchanges the corrected features across groups before prediction. Each group's correction decomposes into explicit prototype contributions before this fusion. (3) We jointly train the forecasting network and operators from scratch and refit each group's prototypes before every training epoch. Slot tracking preserves prototype-operator associations, making each prototype's contribution to local corrections more interpretable. Experiments on GlobalForecast and TyphoonTracking show improved forecasting accuracy, while prototype deletion links local feature contributions to changes in prediction error. This work advances the development of more accurate and interpretable data-driven weather forecasting models. Project page: https://zhixuanli.github.io/projects/li2026protocast

cross Multiple-Camera Tracking of Rigid Objects

Authors: Fr\'ed\'erick Martin, Radu Horaud

Abstract: In this paper we describe a method for tracking rigid objects using one or several cameras. The tracking process consists of aligning a 3-D model representation of an object with image contours by measuring and minimizing the image error between predicted model points and image contours. The tracker behaves like a visual servo loop where the internal and external camera parameters are updated at each new image acquisition. We study in detail the Jacobian matrix associated with this minimization process in the presence of both point-to-point and point-to-contour matches. We establish the minimal number of matches that are needed as well as the singular configurations leading to a rank-defficient Jacobian matrix. We find a mathematical link between the point-to-point and point-to-contour cases. Based on this link we show that the latter has the same kind of singularities than the former. Moreover, we study multiple camera configurations which optimize the robustness of the method in the presence of single-camera singularities, bad, noisy, or missing data. Extensive experiments done with a complex ship part and with up to three cameras validate the method. In particular we show that the tracker may well be used as a camera calibration procedure.

Abstract: World models aim to predict environment dynamics under actions, enabling the prediction of action consequences without executing them in the real world. cross UniVEP: Unifying Velocity and Endpoint Prediction for Robot Manipulation

Authors: Zhengtu Liang, Qiwei Liang, Tao Lin, Shihang Weng, Boyang Cai, Xiangyu Chen, Yikeng Chen, Jiaming Liang, Bohan Tan, Yu Yan, Renjing Xu

Abstract: Flow-matching robot policies usually use one prediction target throughout action generation, even though recovering actions from noise and polishing nearly clean actions bring different challenges. Abstract: The Unitree Go1 is a widely used platform for legged robotics research, yet collecting proprioceptive data from it is unnecessarily difficult: the Unitree SDK is sparsely documented, and ROS1 (Robot Operating System), the Go1's native middleware, is fully deprecated. cross Invertible Transport Between Sample-Only Distributions via Projected Characteristic Functions

Authors: Juan Guti\'errez, Victor Guti\'errez-Garc\'ia, Jos\'e Luis Blanco-Murillo

Abstract: Many useful latent distributions can be sampled but do not have a tractable density. Such distributions may be represented by empirical collections of real-world observations or generated by scientific simulators and black-box generative models. These sample-only priors cannot be used directly in likelihood-based normalizing flows, which require evaluating the prior density. We propose Projected Characteristic-Function matching (PCF), a density-free framework for learning an invertible map between a data distribution and a prior specified only through samples. Our method aligns distributions using projected empirical characteristic functions, eliminating the need for density estimation, adversarial training, or paired observations. To address the non-uniqueness of the transport map, we introduce an optional transport regularizer that selects lower-displacement transformations while preserving endpoint alignment. We benchmark PCF on controlled transports, sample-efficiency studies, and high-dimensional alignment tasks. PCF matches the accuracy of kernel MMD on the controlled benchmark and on raw-pixel MNIST--USPS at a cost linear in the batch size, and it is more sample-efficient, achieving lower endpoint error than MMD in 13 of 15 paired runs across library sizes. By avoiding pairwise kernel evaluation, it reduces discrepancy memory by 29.8% and trains at batch sizes where kernel MMD exceeds GPU memory. These results show that PCF provides accurate and efficient invertible transport, particularly in sample- and memory-limited settings.

cross Learn, Freeze, and Transfer an Activation Function Across Architectures

Authors: Arun Prakash A

Abstract: Transfer learning typically reuses knowledge acquired by a source network through its learned weights. We investigate whether a learned activation function can also be transferred and remain useful in networks trained from scratch. We study this complementary form of transfer through a learn-freeze-transfer protocol, in which the activation is optimized jointly with a source network in a single training run, and is then frozen and reused in target networks trained from scratch. A pointwise activation with globally shared parameters is independent of layer dimensions and can therefore be incorporated into networks of different architectures trained from scratch. We instantiate this protocol with FAct, a five-parameter activation represented by a two-harmonic truncated Fourier series, initialized from a Fourier approximation of GELU and learned in a Vision Transformer (ViT) on ImageNet-1K. When reused in a ViT of the same architecture trained from scratch on ImageNet-1K, the frozen activation (Frozen FAct) improves top-1 accuracy by 1.46 percentage points over GELU. Across four smaller target architectures (standard ViT, Swin Transformer, linear-attention ViT, and MLP-Mixer) and four image-classification datasets, it achieves the highest mean accuracy among eleven activation functions in 15 of 16 settings under a common training configuration, with improvements of up to 1.71 percentage points over the best conventional activation. When the learning rate and weight decay are tuned separately for each activation under the same search budget, Frozen FAct still ranks first in 11 of the 16 settings, second in four, and fourth in one. These results provide empirical evidence that a learned activation function can retain its usefulness when transferred across vision architectures, independently of the weights with which it was learned.

cross SKILL.PNG: Learning Agent Skills in Pixel Space

Authors: Maximilian Seeth, Chengzhi Martin Hu, Marie Kempkes, Paul R\"ottger, Leo Schwinn

Abstract: AI agents are commonly specialized for new tasks and domains using skills, i.e. natural language instructions placed in their context. However, the discrete nature of textual skills prevents continuous optimization. Gradient-based approaches such as finetuning, on the other hand, are typically model-specific, preventing skill transfer across different agents. Since many agents today are built on vision-language models (VLMs), we can address these limitations by introducing Skill.png: a method for obtaining skill-like image inputs whose pixels are optimized with gradient descent through the vision encoder of a frozen VLM. Image skills are compatible with any VLM, and a single image can be optimized jointly for an ensemble of models. We show that Skill.png significantly outperforms state-of-the-art textual skills optimized using frontier LLMs in 9 of 16 settings (across 4 VLMs and 4 benchmarks) while requiring only 64 to 256 image tokens instead of roughly 900 to 2000 text tokens at inference time. Hybrid skills, i.e. text and image skills jointly optimized, further improve agent performance. Moreover, an image optimized with an ensemble of VLMs can successfully transfer across the models in the ensemble. Skill.png thus presents the first agent skill that can be optimized with gradient descent while retaining transfer benefits of text skills. Our code is available at: https://github.com/omseeth/skill.png

URLs: https://github.com/omseeth/skill.png

cross TacInno: Teaching Vision-Language-Action Models the Contact Dynamics Beyond Vision and Action

Authors: Weile Li, Yunfan Lou, Muqing Shi, Zihao Zhang, Zhenbiao Huang, Boxian Yao, Xiyu Zhu, Zhibo Pang

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have recently been equipped with tactile sensing to perceive visually subtle contact events in contact-rich manipulation. However, adding tactile input yields inconsistent gains, and some visuo-tactile VLA models rely little on tactile input, because much of the tactile change, such as the gel deformation during gripper closure, is predictable from visual observations and executed actions. To address this problem, we introduce TacInno, which isolates the contact dynamics that vision and executed actions cannot explain, termed tactile innovation, and uses tactile innovation only as a training target. Specifically, the TacInno tokenizer, conditioned on visual observations and executed actions, compresses the future tactile change into a discrete code, and the policy, TacInno-VLA, learns to predict the code, adding no module for the code at deployment. TacInno-VLA achieves average success rates of 87.5% across six simulation tasks and 70.5% across four real-world tasks, outperforming the strongest visuo-tactile baseline by 10.7 and 9.0 percentage points, respectively. Ablation studies validate the contributions of visual and action conditioning, multi-frame encoding, and auxiliary code prediction. Together, these results demonstrate the effectiveness of tactile innovation as a supervision signal for contact-rich manipulation.

cross Awaken-HI: Scaling Humanoid Intelligence with Whole-Body Human Experience

Authors: EngineAI

Abstract: A central goal of humanoid robotics is for robots to perform the everyday work people do in homes and workplaces. Existing egocentric video datasets cover many of these activities but provide little synchronized whole-body motion data for learning loco-manipulation at scale. We build Awaken-Human, which pairs approximately 50,000 hours of egocentric stereo video with synchronized whole-body human motion and covers more than 100 skills. Building on this, we also introduce Awaken-HI (Humanoid Intelligence), a vision-language-action model for humanoid loco-manipulation that jointly predicts original human motion and retargeted robot actions through a shared action expert. We show that loco-manipulation skills transfer more effectively from people to humanoids when human trajectories directly supervise the shared action expert. Increasing human pretraining data reduces held-out robot action prediction error according to an empirical scaling law and raises the mean success rate on downstream real-robot tasks. After full-corpus pretraining and T800 adaptation, Awaken-HI achieves a mean success rate of 93.75% across eight individual tasks and outperforms GR00T N1.7 and $\pi_{0.5}$ in both individual and long-horizon evaluations. The same policy generalizes to objects unseen during robot post-training and to new pairings of familiar objects and destinations.

cross MemFolder: Structure-Guided Memory Folding for Efficient Agent Memory Management

Authors: Xuyang Liu, Chenwen Lu, Junxi Wang, Siyu Ren, Xinying Lin, Siteng Huang, Hongli Sun, Linfeng Zhang, Honggang Chen

Abstract: Long-term agents accumulate large external memory stores as interactions unfold, increasing the costs of storage, retrieval, and response generation. Efficiency is constrained by two coupled factors: a growing record count increases retrieval overhead, while longer retrieved contexts increase prefill computation and decoding-time attention costs. Existing methods prune or consolidate records, but pruning can discard useful evidence, whereas consolidation may produce fewer yet longer retrieval units. We introduce MemFolder, a structure-guided memory folding framework that sequentially reduces record count and context length. The framework comprises two core components that share memory groups, using cross-record structure to guide both evidence consolidation and compact representation. RecordFolder grounds entity mentions, progressively selects representative yet complementary anchors, and combines semantic and entity affinity with temporal chaining to consolidate compatible records into fewer retrieval units. ContextFolder encodes the original records within these groups into compact text and uses verification feedback to revise encodings when key facts cannot be recovered. Experiments on the three text-based memory benchmarks LoCoMo, PersonaMem, and LongMemEval and the two multimodal benchmarks M3-Bench-web and M3-Bench-robot demonstrate the strong performance of our methods. Notably, on Mem0, RecordFolder retains 99.0% of the original average performance across the three text-based benchmarks with 50% fewer records, exceeding the strongest baseline by 1.9 percentage points in performance retention. Combined with ContextFolder, existing record compression methods further shorten their output records while retaining most of their performance.

cross Mind2Act World: A Diagnostic Benchmark for Embodied Mind, Physical Action, and Mind-Action Composition in Robotic Manipulation

Authors: Yuzhi Huang, Jie Wu, Fanding Huang, Junchao Lin, Weichen Zhang, Ziyi Xiong, Yang Li, Zhengwei Xie, Zhimin Wang, Zhen Liu, Jiazhen Huang, Yuzheng Zhang, Yuche Liu, Hongyi Yang, Chen Tang, Shixiang Tang, Hakan Aktas, Adam Wu, Duo Wu, Kangye Ji, Shuzhao Xie, Huanan Liu, Weijue Bu, Chenxin Li, Xinyuan Wang, Tianyi Bai, Yinan Mao, Jiawei Wang, Minglei Li, Heng Yu, Ehsan Adeli, Minchen Li, Yong-Lu Li, Cengiz \"Oztireli, Huazhe Xu, Chao Yu, Jing Zhang, Shanghang Zhang, Weitao Zhou, Wenwu Zhu, Cheng Chi, Jingyan Jiang, Zhi Wang

Abstract: Embodied intelligence aims to let robots perform complex physical tasks with human-like adaptability and reliability, which requires three complementary capabilities: history-based cognitive reasoning, precise and timely physical execution, and the composition of reasoning and execution through environmental feedback. Yet existing benchmarks either evaluate reasoning and execution within the same tasks or assess them separately without their composition, so they reveal little about whether a failure comes from reasoning, execution, or their composition. We introduce Mind2Act World, a diagnostic benchmark of 30 simulated manipulation tasks from five real-world application domains. Each task has three difficulty levels and two scene settings (Clean and Random), yielding 180 configurations. The tasks form three views: Mind tests decisions based on past observations, Act tests precise and timely execution, and Mind2Act combines both in closed-loop tasks where action outcomes guide later decisions. Comparing the three views shows whether a system lacks a capability or fails to compose capabilities it already has. A diagnosis harness further attributes the failed episodes of three representative systems to Mind, Act, or their composition and summarizes each system in a profile card. Our evaluation of multimodal agents, vision-language-action models (VLAs), and world-action models (WAMs) shows that competence in a single view does not carry over to Mind2Act tasks. Even the strongest agent succeeds in 52.7% of Mind and 39.0% of Act episodes but in only 12.0% of Mind2Act episodes. The strongest agent retains task state but struggles with sustained contact, whereas end-to-end policies execute practiced motions but retain little task state. These findings point to composing reasoning and execution through environmental feedback as the main open problem.

cross Visual Attribution Limits and Graded Provenance Auditing in Commercial Video-Generation APIs

Authors: Haocheng Mei, Yuchuan Tian, Yingte Shu, Chao Xu, Kai Han, Hanting Chen, Yunhe Wang

Abstract: Commercial video-generation APIs bill by model and tier, while intermediaries could substitute cheaper models, lower tiers, or cached clips. What can a delivered file establish about its origin? We combine measurements of passive attribution and cryptographic provenance into a graded audit. We test attribution probes and zero-shot detectors under re-encoding, downscaling, and frame-rate changes, and survey C2PA and GB 45438 metadata across 19 commercial endpoints. Only authenticated provenance supports accusations: tier contradictions or duplicate signed instances under fresh-generation contracts, excluding retries and idempotent returns. Unverifiable signed-endpoint deliveries trigger original-file demands under pass-through clauses; archive-reuse and delivery-parameter screens support review only. Six-model container and bitstream accuracy falls from 83.9% on raw files to 45.3% after uniform re-encoding, using different feature sets. Subsampling real references from 24 to 3 fps raises pooled D3 AUROC from 0.845 to 0.988. Only BytePlus authenticates tier claims under our pinned policy, including 187/187 audited deliveries across three Seedance tiers; tested remuxing and re-encoding leave processed Seedance files unverifiable. The audit identifies authenticated contradictions in 100/100 raw 720p signed tier-swap cases, with zero observed false flags on the tested honest pools.

cross Text-Aware, Vision-Blind: How Multimodal Image Editing Systems Handle Unsafe Inputs

Authors: Ziqing Yang, Yixin Wu, Michael Backes, Yang Zhang

Abstract: Multimodal image generation systems have made rapid progress and increasingly emphasize content safety. However, their safety mechanisms tend to rely on a single modality and lack joint consideration of both visual content and text prompts, particularly in instruction-guided image editing, where the uploaded image may itself carry risk. We thus introduce EditSafetyBench, a benchmark that measures system-level safety awareness of advanced image editing systems, containing 5,940 image-instruction pairs, covering safe and unsafe images across 11 risk categories and three edit intents. We then probe the benchmark dataset on four systems, including Gemini, GPT, Qwen API, and Qwen Local. Based on our evaluation, we observe a systematic text-vision safety mismatch: Gemini and GPT refuse 87.1\% and 81.6\% cases containing unsafe-intent editing prompts, while only refusing 24.2\% and 22.4\% cases with both unsafe images and neutral instructions. At the category level, most risks are considered inconsistently by different systems, e.g., GPT allows 70.4\% editing attempts of unsafe political content while Gemini only allows 34.8\%. Notably, sexual content shows the strongest visual sensitivity, likely benefiting from mature NSFW identification works. Our findings highlight the need for safety guardrails that jointly reason over both visual and textual modalities in image editing systems.

cross Probing the "Conscious" Mind: Selective Access and Global Workspace in Vision-Language Models

Authors: Jiahao Huang, Jun Wang

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) integrate visual and textual information, but their outputs do not reveal internal concept representations. Global workspace theory (GWT) proposes that information is selected from parallel brain processes, integrated in a shared workspace, and made available to specialised processes. We introduce the Multimodal Concept Lens (MC-Lens), a multimodal Jacobian lens, to study these functions in VLM concept processing. Its vision encoder and language decoder lenses map intermediate states to a shared semantic space defined by the decoder's output vocabulary to compare concept accessibility across modalities. MC-Lens supports internal concept interventions to examine the role of accessed concepts in downstream computation. (i) Accessibility and selective access. Multimodal concept information is readable in intermediate states and selectively accessible as tasks require. We extend "readout carriers" from one name token to a token set to better capture and express concept information and support counterfactual experiments. (ii) Concepts in downstream computation. Counterfactual experiments show that accessed concept information participates in downstream computation and reasoning. (iii) Development of cross-modal alignment and selective access. We develop two J-space metrics tracking the development of cross-modal concept alignment more clearly than hidden state cosine in controlled VLM training. The experiment also shows that reliable selective access develops later than concept accessibility. (iv) Shared functions across models. Across VLM families and sizes, we find shared patterns of concept accessibility, query-dependent selectivity and cross-task intervention effects despite differences in internal organisation. These findings offer an experimental basis to study GWT in multimodal artificial systems and how it can guide cross-modal alignment improvements.

cross AgentRoboCritic: Understanding the Capability of Frontier Agents on Direct Robot Control

Authors: Yiyang Du, Yuxuan Kuang, Qitao Zhao, Yixuan Wang, Zhanqiu Guo, Yi Xu, Xin Li, Wenbin He, Liu Ren, Chenyan Xiong

Abstract: Frontier multimodal language agents such as GPT-6 Astra have demonstrated strong capabilities in controlling robots directly via low-level numerical actions without high-level primitives. However, existing benchmarks report only episode-level success rates, which show neither how these agents succeed nor where they fail. In this paper, we present a systematic action-level analysis of frontier agents on direct robot control across 12 manipulation benchmarks. We introduce AgentRoboCritic, an intent-grounded critique framework in which the policy agent states a local intent alongside each action, and a critic evaluates the action's outcome and failure mode against this intent. Our action-level analysis demonstrates the promising capabilities of frontier agents on direct robot control, including closed-loop recovery from local execution failures, reliable semantic grounding across diverse scenes, and strong zero-shot manipulation without task-specific adaptation. It also identifies their current limitations, including task-relevant physical execution bottlenecks such as grasping and alignment, and uneven recovery across failure modes. Finally, we validate our critic on a 400-action human-annotated benchmark and release a dataset of 12,844 annotated actions alongside a fine-tuned open-source critic, Muse-Glimmer-30B, whose failure-mode accuracy rises from 32.58% to 74.24%, enabling low-cost action-level analysis.

cross PlayCraft: Designing Engaging Games from Agent Play Experience

Authors: Weihao Cheng

Abstract: Recent AI agents can generate playable games, but these games are not necessarily engaging. Mastery is a key source of engagement, yet designing for it requires extensive human play and tuning. We propose PlayCraft, an automated framework that uses a vision-based agent to play games and draws on its detailed play experience to revise them for mastery. Starting from an initial game, PlayCraft alternates between playing and crafting. In the playing stage, a player agent watches the game screen, uses the controls, and pursues the game objective over repeated episodes while retaining memory. After each episode, it reports its progress, outcomes, decisions, and reflections. In the crafting stage, a crafter agent uses these reports to revise the game. It estimates player abilities, predicts performance at different proficiency levels, and compares those predictions with a target learning progression. It diagnoses the game and addresses the gaps by giving players meaningful opportunities to improve through better decisions and execution. We evaluate games in fresh runs with player agents across three intelligence settings, measuring learning gain and learning slope. Compared with the initial games, PlayCraft yields larger learning gains and steeper learning slopes in most games. It also outperforms the baselines on the macro-average of each metric at every intelligence setting.

replace Open-Vocabulary Scene Text Recognition via Pseudo-Image Labeling and Margin Loss

Authors: Xuhua Ren, Hengcan Shi, Jin Li

Abstract: Scene text recognition is an important and challenging task in computer vision. However, most prior works focus on recognizing pre-defined words, while there are various out-of-vocabulary (OOV) words in real-world applications. In this paper, we propose a novel open-vocabulary text recognition framework, Pseudo-OCR, to recognize OOV words. The key challenge in this task is the lack of OOV training data. To solve this problem, we first propose a pseudo label generation module that leverages character detection and image inpainting to produce substantial pseudo OOV training data from real-world images. Unlike previous synthetic data, our pseudo OOV data contains real characters and backgrounds to simulate real-world applications. Secondly, to reduce noises in pseudo data, we present a semantic checking mechanism to filter semantically meaningful data. Thirdly, we introduce a quality-aware margin loss to boost the training with pseudo data. Our loss includes a margin-based part to enhance the classification ability, and a quality-aware part to penalize low-quality samples in both real and pseudo data. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our approach outperforms the state-of-the-art on eight datasets and achieves the first rank in the ICDAR2022 challenge.

Authors: Yuanhao Cai, Zihao Xiao, Yixun Liang, Minghan Qin, Yulun Zhang, Xiaokang Yang, Yaoyao Liu, Alan Yuille

Abstract: High dynamic range (HDR) novel view synthesis (NVS) aims to create photorealistic images from novel viewpoints using HDR imaging techniques. The rendered HDR images capture a wider range of brightness levels containing more details of the scene than normal low dynamic range (LDR) images. Existing HDR NVS methods are mainly based on NeRF. They suffer from long training time and slow inference speed. In this paper, we propose a new framework, High Dynamic Range Gaussian Splatting (HDR-GS), which can efficiently render novel HDR views and reconstruct LDR images with a user input exposure time. Specifically, we design a Dual Dynamic Range (DDR) Gaussian point cloud model that uses spherical harmonics to fit HDR color and employs an MLP-based tone-mapper to render LDR color. The HDR and LDR colors are then fed into two Parallel Differentiable Rasterization (PDR) processes to reconstruct HDR and LDR views. To establish the data foundation for the research of 3D Gaussian splatting-based methods in HDR NVS, we recalibrate the camera parameters and compute the initial positions for Gaussian point clouds. Experiments demonstrate that our HDR-GS surpasses the state-of-the-art NeRF-based method by 3.84 and 1.91 dB on LDR and HDR NVS while enjoying 1000x inference speed and only requiring 6.3% training time. Code and recalibrated data will be publicly available at https://github.com/caiyuanhao1998/HDR-GS . Our project page at https://caiyuanhao1998.github.io/project/HDR-GS/ shows more video and interactive results. An online Gradio demo is also available on HuggingFace at https://huggingface.co/spaces/CaiYuanhao/HDR-GS

Authors: Shuchang Zhang, Hongxia Wang

Abstract: In recent years Plug-and-Play (PnP) methods have achieved state-of-the-art performance in inverse imaging problems by replacing proximal operators with denoisers. Abstract: Enabling object manipulation based on a single static image, from geometric editing to animation, remains a significant challenge. replace Breaking the Discretization Barrier of Continuous Physics Simulation Learning

Authors: Fan Xu, Hao Wu, Nan Wang, Lilan Peng, Kun Wang, Wei Gong, Xibin Zhao

Abstract: The modeling of complicated time-evolving physical dynamics from partial observations is a long-standing challenge. Particularly, observations can be sparsely distributed in a seemingly random or unstructured manner, making it difficult to capture highly nonlinear features in a variety of scientific and engineering problems. However, existing data-driven approaches are often constrained by fixed spatial and temporal discretization. While some researchers attempt to achieve spatio-temporal continuity by designing novel strategies, they either overly rely on traditional numerical methods or fail to truly overcome the limitations imposed by discretization. To address these, we propose CoPS, a purely data-driven methods, to effectively model continuous physics simulation from partial observations. Specifically, we employ multiplicative filter network to fuse and encode spatial information with the corresponding observations. Then we customize geometric grids and use message-passing mechanism to map features from original spatial domain to the customized grids. Subsequently, CoPS models continuous-time dynamics by designing multi-scale graph ODEs, while introducing a Markov-based neural auto-correction module to assist and constrain the continuous extrapolations. Comprehensive experiments demonstrate that CoPS advances the state-of-the-art methods in space-time continuous modeling across various scenarios.

Authors: Juan Guti\'errez, Victor Guti\'errez-Garc\'ia, Jos\'e Luis Blanco-Murillo

Abstract: AI-generated Pre-Annotations can accelerate video labeling, but they may also anchor annotators to model priors and suppress disagreement that is valuable for pluralistic dataset construction. Overall, the results suggest that Pre-Annotations acted mainly as editable temporal scaffolds in this pilot setting, while leaving open the possibility of subtler semantic anchoring effects. replace LoC-Path: Learning to Compress for Pathology Multimodal Large Language Models

Authors: Qingqiao Hu, Weimin Lyu, Meilong Xu, Xiaoling Hu, Kehan Qi, Saumya Gupta, Jiawei Zhou, Chao Chen

Abstract: Whole Slide Image (WSI) multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are difficult to build and deploy because gigapixel slides induce thousands of highly redundant visual tokens that must be learned from sparse slide-level language supervision. Existing slide-level pathology MLLMs typically combine heavy slide-level encoders with long visual prefixes, making development and deployment expensive under limited computational resources. We revisit this regime and show that WSI tile features are highly redundant at both global and local scales, while task-relevant evidence is sparse and query-dependent. We therefore introduce LoC-Path, a resource-efficient slide-level MLLM that compresses before fusion. LoC-Path uses a sparse token merger and a masked-autoencoder (MAE)-pretrained resampler to replace expensive slide-level encoding with a compact latent interface. A token importance scorer selects the most relevant latents and cross-attention routing adapters fuse them into a few LLM decoder layers. This design lowers both multimodal tuning cost and inference-time latency/memory by avoiding heavy slide-level encoding and long visual prefixes. Extensive experiments show that the inference TFLOPs of LoC-Path are 97% lower and inference memory is also 39% lower. LoC-Path enables efficient end-to-end development and deployment under limited computational resources while remaining competitive with prior slide-level MLLMs. Code is available at https://github.com/WinstonHuTiger/loc_path.

Abstract: Posing 3D characters is a fundamental task in computer graphics. However, existing paradigms, ranging from traditional auto-rigging to recent pose-conditioned generative models, frequently struggle with inaccurate skinning weights, fixed mesh topologies, and poor pose conformance. These challenges have become particularly pronounced with the recent explosion of AI-generated 3D assets, which often exhibit flawed structures and fused geometry. To address these issues, we introduce \textbf{Make-It-Poseable}, a novel feed-forward framework that reformulates character posing as a skinning-free latent-space transformation problem. By decoupling shape deformation from the constraints of fixed mesh connectivity, our method directly operates on compact latent representations to reconstruct characters in target poses.
